View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges that contains neither self-loops nor double edges.

Here, a self-loop is an edge where a_i = b_i (1≤i≤M), and double edges are two edges where (a_i,b_i)=(a_j,b_j) or (a_i,b_i)=(b_j,a_j) (1≤i<j≤M).

How many different paths start from vertex 1 and visit all the vertices exactly once?

Here, the endpoints of a path are considered visited.  
For example, let us assume that the following undirected graph shown in Figure 1 is given.
Figure 1: an example of an undirected graph
The following path shown in Figure 2 satisfies the condition.
Figure 2: an example of a path that satisfies the condition
However, the following path shown in Figure 3 does not satisfy the condition, because it does not visit all the vertices.
Figure 3: an example of a path that does not satisfy the condition
Neither the following path shown in Figure 4, because it does not start from vertex 1.
Figure 4: another example of a path that does not satisfy the condition

-----Constraints-----
 - 2≦N≦8 
 - 0≦M≦N(N-1)/2 
 - 1≦a_i<b_i≦N 
 - The given graph contains neither self-loops nor double edges.

-----Input-----
The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M  
a_1 b_1  
a_2 b_2
:  
a_M b_M  

-----Output-----
Print the number of the different paths that start from vertex 1 and visit all the vertices exactly once.

-----Sample Input-----
3 3
1 2
1 3
2 3

-----Sample Output-----
2

The given graph is shown in the following figure:
The following two paths satisfy the condition:

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once (Hamiltonian path)
   - The path must be connected through edges that exist in the graph

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially asking for the number of Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1
2. Since N ≤ 8, we can use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths
3. We need to ensure we visit each vertex exactly once
4. We can only move between vertices if there's an edge between them

## Solution Approach

1. Build the adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use DFS/backtracking to generate all paths starting from vertex 1
3. For each path, check if it visits all N vertices exactly once
4. Count such valid paths

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Read input and build the adjacency list
2. Use recursive function to explore all paths from vertex 1
3. Keep track of visited vertices
4. When we reach a path of length N (visiting all vertices), increment counter
5. Backtrack to explore other possibilities

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)
    
    # Count paths starting from vertex 1 that visit all vertices exactly once
    def dfs(vertex, visited):
        # Mark current vertex as visited
        visited[vertex] = True
        
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited) == N + 1:  # All vertices from 1 to N are visited
            return 1
        
        count = 0
        # Try to visit each neighbor
        for neighbor in adj[vertex]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                count += dfs(neighbor, visited)
        
        # Backtrack - unmark current vertex (though we don't need to since we're not using it)
        # Actually, we don't need to unmark since we're passing by value in recursive calls
        # The visited array is modified in each recursive call, and we're not reusing it
        
        return count
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    # We need to initialize visited array
    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    visited[1] = True
    
    # We need to find all paths that visit all vertices
    # The above DFS will count all paths from 1 to any vertex, but we need to ensure all vertices are visited
    # The condition `if len(visited) == N + 1` is not quite right because we're not properly tracking the count
    
    # Let me fix this - we need to track how many vertices are visited
    def dfs_correct(vertex, visited_set):
        # Mark current vertex as visited
        visited_set.add(vertex)
        
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited_set) == N:
            return 1
        
        count = 0
        # Try to visit each neighbor
        for neighbor in adj[vertex]:
            if neighbor not in visited_set:
                count += dfs_correct(neighbor, visited_set)
        
        return count
    
    # Start from vertex 1
    result = dfs_correct(1, set([1]))
    print(result)

solve()
```

Wait, I see an issue with my logic. Let me reconsider.

Actually, the above DFS will work correctly. Let me trace through the sample:
- N=3, M=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3)
- We start at vertex 1, mark it as visited
- From vertex 1, we can go to 2 or 3

Case 1: 1 → 2
- From 2, we can go to 1 (already visited) or 3 (not visited)
- 1 → 2 → 3: this visits all vertices (1,2,3) → count += 1

Case 2: 1 → 3  
- From 3, we can go to 1 (already visited) or 2 (not visited)
- 1 → 3 → 2: this visits all vertices (1,3,2) → count += 1

Total count = 2, which matches the expected output.

The solution is correct.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once (Hamiltonian path)
   - The endpoints are considered visited

This is essentially asking for the number of Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1.

## Approach

Since N ≤ 8, we can use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths starting from vertex 1, ensuring we visit each vertex exactly once.

Steps:
1. Build an adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use DFS/backtracking to generate all paths starting from vertex 1
3. For each path, check if it visits all N vertices exactly once
4. Count such valid paths

## Algorithm Details

- Start at vertex 1
- At each step, try to go to any unvisited neighbor
- Continue until we have visited all vertices
- When we've visited all vertices, we have found a valid path

## Optimization Note

Since we need to visit all vertices exactly once, we can:
1. Keep track of visited vertices using a set
2. Only explore neighbors that haven't been visited yet
3. When we've visited N vertices, we've found a valid path

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count paths starting from vertex 1 that visit all vertices exactly once
    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    count = 0
    
    def dfs(current, remaining):
        nonlocal count
        
        # Mark current vertex as visited
        visited[current] = True
        
        # If all vertices are visited, we found a valid path
        if remaining == 0:
            count += 1
            return
        
        # Try all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                # Go to neighbor and reduce remaining vertices
                dfs(neighbor, remaining - 1)
        
        # Backtrack - unmark current vertex (though we don't need to since we're not returning)
        # Actually, we don't need to unmark because we're using the visited array and will 
        # only visit each vertex once in the DFS tree
        
        # For backtracking, we don't need to unmark because we're not reusing the same path
        # The recursion will naturally handle it as we only visit each vertex once
        # But actually, we need to backtracking to allow trying different paths
        # So we should unmark - but since we're not doing it here, let's fix this
        
        # Wait, I see the issue - we need to unmark to allow backtracking
        # But our recursion structure doesn't explicitly do that
        # Actually, in this DFS, we're not properly unmarking
        
        # Let me restructure - we'll do proper backtracking in the recursion call
    
    # Actually, let me rewrite the DFS to properly handle backtracking
    
    def dfs_with_backtracking(current, visited_set):
        nonlocal count
        
        # Add current vertex to visited set
        visited_set.add(current)
        
        # If we've visited all vertices, we have a valid path
        if len(visited_set) == N:
            count += 1
            return
        
        # Try all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if neighbor not in visited_set:
                dfs_with_backtracking(neighbor, visited_set.copy())  # Copy to avoid modifying original
        
        # Don't need to unmark since we're using a copy
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    dfs_with_backtracking(1, set())
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

```python
        visited.add(current)
        
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited) == N:
            count += 1
            return
        
        # Try all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                dfs(neighbor, visited.copy())  # Create a copy of visited set
        
        # No need to unmark since we use copies
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    dfs(1, set())
    
    print(count)
```

Actually, creating a copy of the set in each recursive call might be inefficient for larger inputs, but since N is at most 8, it's acceptable.

However, there's a simpler approach using a visited array:

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count paths starting from vertex 1 that visit all vertices exactly once
    count = 0
    
    def dfs(current, visited):
        nonlocal count
        
        # Mark current vertex as visited
        visited[current] = True
        
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if sum(visited[1:N+1]) == N:
            count += 1
        
        # Try all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor, visited)
        
        # Backtrack - unmark current vertex
        visited[current] = False
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    visited[1] = True  # start from vertex 1
    
    dfs(1, visited)
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

This version is more efficient and clearer.

Let me test with the sample:
- N=3, M=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3)
- From vertex 1, we can go to 2 or 3
- If we go to 2, then from 2 we can go to 3 (since 1 is already visited)
- If we go to 3, then from 3 we can go to 2 (since 1 is already visited)
- So we get two paths: 1-2-3 and 1-3-2

That matches the expected output of 2.

```python
    global count

    visited[current] = True
    
    # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
    if sum(visited[1:N+1]) == N:

    # Try all neighbors
    for neighbor in graph[current]:
        if not visited[neighbor]:
            dfs(neighbor, visited)
    
    # Backtrack - unmark current vertex
    visited[current] = False

# Start DFS from vertex 1

dfs(1, visited)

print(count)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once (Hamiltonian path)
   - The endpoints are considered visited

Since N ≤ 8, we can use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths.

## Approach

1. Build the adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use backtracking to generate all paths starting from vertex 1
3. For each path, check if it visits all N vertices exactly once
4. Count such valid paths

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Parse input**: Read N, M and the edges
2. **Build adjacency list**: Create a graph where each vertex knows its neighbors
3. **Backtracking**: Start from vertex 1, try to visit each unvisited neighbor
4. **Track visited vertices**: Use a set to keep track of visited vertices
5. **Check completeness**: When we've visited all vertices, count it as a valid path

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count valid paths starting from vertex 1 that visit all vertices exactly once
    def backtrack(current, visited):
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited) == N:
            return 1
        
        total_paths = 0
        # Try to go to each unvisited neighbor
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                total_paths += backtrack(neighbor, visited)
                visited.remove(neighbor)  # backtrack
        
        return total_paths
    
    # Start backtracking from vertex 1
    # Initially, vertex 1 is visited
    visited = {1}
    result = backtrack(1, visited)
    
    return result

print(solve())
```

## Verification with Sample

Sample input: N=3, M=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3)

Graph structure:
- Vertex 1: connected to 2, 3
- Vertex 2: connected to 1, 3  
- Vertex 3: connected to 1, 2

Valid paths starting from 1 that visit all vertices:
1. 1 → 2 → 3
2. 1 → 3 → 2

That's 2 paths, which matches the expected output.

The solution uses backtracking to explore all possible paths while ensuring each vertex is visited exactly once. The time complexity is reasonable for N ≤ 8.

            return 1

                visited.remove(neighbor)  # backtrack
        
        return total_paths
    
    # Start backtracking from vertex 1
    # Initially, vertex 1 is visited
    visited = {1}
    result = backtrack(1, visited)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to find the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once
   - The endpoints are considered visited
3. This is essentially asking for the number of Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1.

## Key Observations

1. Since the graph has at most 8 vertices (N ≤ 8), we can use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths
2. We need to count paths that start from vertex 1 and visit all vertices exactly once
3. Since the graph is undirected, if there's an edge between u and v, there's also an edge between v and u
4. We need to check connectivity - if vertex 1 is not connected to some other vertices, those vertices cannot be visited

## Approach

1. Build the adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use backtracking to generate all possible paths starting from vertex 1
3. For each path, check if it visits all vertices exactly once
4. Count valid paths

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input and build adjacency list
2. Use DFS/backtracking to explore all paths starting from vertex 1
3. Keep track of visited vertices
4. When we have visited all vertices (N vertices total), count it as a valid path

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1
    def dfs(current, visited):
        # Mark current vertex as visited
        visited[current] = True
        
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited) - visited.count(False) == N:
            return 1
        
        count = 0
        # Explore all neighbors
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                count += dfs(neighbor, visited)
        
        # Backtrack - unmark current vertex (it will be unmarked by the recursive call)
        # Actually, we don't need to explicitly unmark since visited array is passed by reference
        # But we need to handle that we're not modifying the original array incorrectly
        
        # The above approach has an issue - we're modifying visited array
        # Let's fix it by passing a mutable visited array and not modifying it in recursion
        
        # Actually, let's rewrite the DFS to avoid modifying visited array in calls
        return count
    
    # Actually, let's use a cleaner approach
    def count_paths():
        # Use backtracking with visited set
        path_count = 0
        
        def backtrack(current, visited_set):
            nonlocal path_count
            
            # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
            if len(visited_set) == N:
                path_count += 1
                return
            
            # Try all neighbors
            for neighbor in graph[current]:
                if neighbor not in visited_set:
                    visited_set.add(neighbor)
                    backtrack(neighbor, visited_set)
                    visited_set.remove(neighbor)  # Backtrack
        
        # Start from vertex 1
        visited_set = {1}
        backtrack(1, visited_set)
        
        return path_count
    
    result = count_paths()
    print(result)

solve()
```

You are given an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges that contains neither self-loops nor double edges.

Here, a self-loop is an edge where a_i = b_i (1≤i≤M), and double edges are two edges where (a_i,b_i)=(a_j,b_j) or (a_i,b_i)=(b_j,a_j) (1≤i<j≤M).

How many different paths start from vertex 1 and visit all the vertices exactly once?

Here, the endpoints of a path are considered visited.  
For example, let us assume that the following undirected graph shown in Figure 1 is given.
Figure 1: an example of an undirected graph
The following path shown in Figure 2 satisfies the condition.
Figure 2: an example of a path that satisfies the condition
However, the following path shown in Figure 3 does not satisfy the condition, because it does not visit all the vertices.
Figure 3: an example of a path that does not satisfy the condition
Neither the following path shown in Figure 4, because it does not start from vertex 1.
Figure 4: another example of a path that does not satisfy the condition

-----Constraints-----
 - 2≦N≦8 
 - 0≦M≦N(N-1)/2 
 - 1≦a_i<b_i≦N 
 - The given graph contains neither self-loops nor double edges.

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M

a_2 b_2
:

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once (Hamiltonian path)
   - Since it's undirected, we can traverse in either direction between connected vertices

## Key Observations

1. This is asking for the number of Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1
2. Since N ≤ 8, we can use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths
3. We need to ensure we visit each vertex exactly once
4. We can only move between adjacent vertices (connected by an edge)

## Algorithm Approach

1. Build an adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use backtracking to explore all paths starting from vertex 1
3. At each step, try to move to any unvisited neighbor
4. When all vertices are visited, count it as a valid path

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input and build adjacency list
2. Use DFS/backtracking to explore all valid paths
3. Keep track of visited vertices to ensure we don't visit the same vertex twice
4. Start from vertex 1 and only consider valid edges to unvisited neighbors

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]  # 1-indexed
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)  # undirected graph
    
    # Count paths that start from vertex 1 and visit all vertices exactly once
    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    count = 0
    
    def dfs(current, visited):
        nonlocal count
        # Mark current vertex as visited
        visited[current] = True
        
        # If all vertices are visited, we found a valid path
        if all(visited[i] for i in range(1, N + 1)):
            count += 1
            # Backtrack: unmark current vertex (though we're not going to visit it again)
            visited[current] = False
            return
        
        # Try all neighbors
        for neighbor in adj[current]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor, visited)
        
        # Backtrack: unmark current vertex
        visited[current] = False
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    visited[1] = True
    dfs(1, visited)
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once
   - The endpoints are considered visited

This is essentially asking for the number of **Hamiltonian paths** starting from vertex 1.

## Key Insights

1. Since N ≤ 8, we can use brute force with backtracking or recursion
2. We need to explore all possible paths that start at vertex 1 and visit every vertex exactly once
3. At each step, we can only move to a neighboring vertex that hasn't been visited yet
4. We need to count all such valid paths

## Algorithm Approach

1. Build the adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths starting from vertex 1
3. For each path, check if it visits all N vertices exactly once
4. Count such valid paths

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Parse input** to build the graph
2. **Build adjacency list** from edges
3. **Use recursion/backtracking** to find all Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1
4. **Count valid paths** that visit all vertices

Let me trace through the sample:
- N=3, M=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3)
- Graph is complete (all vertices connected)
- Paths starting from vertex 1 that visit all vertices:
  - 1 → 2 → 3
  - 1 → 3 → 2
- So answer is 2

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1
    def backtrack(current, visited):
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited) == N:
            return 1
        
        total_paths = 0
        # Try all neighbors of current vertex
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                total_paths += backtrack(neighbor, visited)
                visited.remove(neighbor)
        
        return total_paths
    
    # Start backtracking from vertex 1
    visited = {1}
    result = backtrack(1, visited)
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once (Hamiltonian path)
   - The endpoints are considered visited

Since N ≤ 8, we can use backtracking (DFS) to explore all possible paths.

## Approach

1. Build an adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use DFS/backtracking to generate all paths starting from vertex 1
3. At each step, try to visit unvisited vertices
4. When we've visited all N vertices, count it as a valid path
5. Since we start from vertex 1 and must visit all vertices exactly once, this is essentially finding all Hamiltonian paths from vertex 1

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read input and build adjacency list
2. Use DFS with backtracking:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Keep track of visited vertices
   - At each step, try all adjacent unvisited vertices
   - When all vertices are visited, increment counter
3. Return the counter

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)
    
    # Count valid paths using DFS backtracking
    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    count = 0
    
    def dfs(vertex):
        nonlocal count
        visited[vertex] = True
        
        # If all vertices are visited, we found a valid path
        if all(visited[i] for i in range(1, N + 1)):
            count += 1
        
        # Try all adjacent vertices
        for neighbor in adj[vertex]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor)
        
        # Backtrack
        visited[vertex] = False
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    dfs(1)
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the solution:

1. **Input parsing**: Read N, M and build adjacency list where each vertex stores its neighbors.

2. **DFS with backtracking**: 
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Mark current vertex as visited
   - Check if all vertices are visited (terminal condition)
   - For each unvisited neighbor, recursively explore
   - After exploration, backtrack by unmarking the current vertex

3. **Counting**: Each time we visit all N vertices starting from vertex 1, we increment our counter.

4. **Why this works**: Since we're visiting each vertex exactly once and starting from vertex 1, we're effectively counting Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1.

For the sample input (N=3, M=3, edges: 1-2, 1-3, 2-3):
- Path 1: 1 → 2 → 3
- Path 2: 1 → 3 → 2
- Both visit all vertices exactly once and start from vertex 1
- So answer is 2

The algorithm has time complexity O(N!) in the worst case (when the graph is complete), but since N ≤ 8, this is efficient enough.

def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)
    
    # Count valid paths using DFS backtracking
    visited = [False] * (N + 1)
    count = 0
    
    def dfs(vertex):
        nonlocal count
        visited[vertex] = True
        
        # If all vertices are visited, we found a valid path
        if all(visited[i] for i in range(1, N + 1)):
            count += 1
        
        # Try all adjacent vertices
        for neighbor in adj[vertex]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor)
        
        # Backtrack
        visited[vertex] = False
    
    # Start DFS from vertex 1
    dfs(1)
    
    print(count)

solve()

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected unweighted graph with N vertices and M edges
2. We need to count the number of paths that:
   - Start from vertex 1
   - Visit all vertices exactly once (Hamiltonian path)
   - The endpoints are considered visited

This is essentially asking for the number of Hamiltonian paths starting from vertex 1.

## Key Insights

1. Since N ≤ 8, we can use backtracking/DFS to explore all possible paths
2. We need to generate all possible permutations of vertices that start with vertex 1
3. For each permutation, we need to check if consecutive vertices are connected by an edge
4. Since it's an undirected graph, if (a,b) is an edge, then (b,a) is also an edge

## Algorithm Approach

1. Build an adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Use DFS/backtracking to explore all paths starting from vertex 1
3. At each step, only move to adjacent vertices that haven't been visited yet
4. When we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Parse input to build the graph
2. Create adjacency list from edges
3. Use backtracking to count valid paths that visit all vertices exactly once
4. The path must start at vertex 1

Let me trace through the sample:
- N=3, M=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3)
- All vertices are connected, so we can form paths from 1 to any other vertex
- Paths starting from 1 that visit all vertices:
  - 1 → 2 → 3
  - 1 → 3 → 2
- Count = 2

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)  # undirected graph
    
    # Count paths starting from vertex 1 that visit all vertices exactly once
    def backtrack(current, visited):
        # If we've visited all vertices, we found a valid path
        if len(visited) == N:
            return 1
        
        total_paths = 0
        # Try to go to each unvisited neighbor
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                visited.add(neighbor)
                total_paths += backtrack(neighbor, visited)
                visited.remove(neighbor)  # backtrack
        
        return total_paths
    
    # Start backtracking from vertex 1
    visited = {1}
    result = backtrack(1, visited)
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

The algorithm works as follows:

1. **Graph Construction**: We build an adjacency list where each vertex stores its neighbors
2. **Backtracking**: 
   - Start from vertex 1 with it in the visited set
   - For each current vertex, try to go to each unvisited neighbor
   - Recursively explore from that neighbor
   - When we've visited all N vertices, we've found a valid path
   - Use a set for visited vertices for O(1) lookup
   - After exploring a branch, remove the vertex from visited to backtrack

Time complexity: O(N! / (N-s!) * C) where C is the number of connected components, but in practice it's manageable for N ≤ 8.

The solution correctly handles all constraints and edge cases.

4 3 2 1

NO

In the second sample we can sort the array if we swap elements 1 and 3, so we need 1 swap operation to sort the array. Thus, the answer is "YES".

In the third sample we can't sort the array in more than one swap operation, so the answer is "NO".

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array that was originally sorted in non-decreasing order
2. Someone might have swapped some elements (possibly two elements)
3. We need to determine if the array can be sorted with at most one swap operation
4. If yes, output "YES", otherwise "NO"

## Key Insights

1. If the array is already sorted, we need 0 swaps → answer is "YES"
2. If we can sort the array with exactly 1 swap, answer is "YES"
3. If we need more than 1 swap to sort the array, answer is "NO"

## Approach

1. First, check if the array is already sorted. If yes, return "YES"
2. If not sorted, we need to find if there's exactly one swap that can sort the array
3. To find if one swap can fix it, we can:
   - Find all positions where the array differs from the sorted version
   - If there are 0 differences → already sorted
   - If there are 2 differences → swapping those two positions will fix it
   - If there are more than 2 differences → cannot be fixed by one swap

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Create a sorted version of the array
2. Compare the original array with the sorted array to find mismatched positions
3. Count how many positions differ
4. If 0 or 2 positions differ, we can sort with at most one swap → "YES"
5. If more than 2 positions differ, we cannot sort with one swap → "NO"

Wait, I need to be more careful. The problem says "can be sorted in no more than one operation of swapping elements" - this means 0 or 1 swap.

But there's a catch: what if the array has duplicates? For example, if we have two identical elements in wrong positions, swapping them won't change anything.

However, the key insight is that we need to find positions where elements are wrong. If there are exactly 2 positions where the element doesn't match the sorted array, then swapping those two positions will fix it.

But what if there are duplicates? For example, array [1, 3, 2, 3]. The sorted version is [1, 2, 3, 3]. The differences are at position 1 and 2, so we can swap positions 1 and 2 to get [1, 2, 3, 3] - this works.

Actually, the condition is: the number of positions where original[i] != sorted[i] should be either 0 or 2.

Why 2? Because swapping two elements changes exactly two positions. If we have 0 mismatches, we're already sorted. If we have 2 mismatches, swapping those two positions will fix both. If we have more than 2 mismatches, we need more than one swap.

However, what if the mismatches involve duplicate values? For example, if we have [1, 2, 1, 3] and sorted is [1, 1, 2, 3]. The mismatches are at position 1 and 2. Position 1 has 2 (should be 1), position 2 has 1 (should be 2). Swapping them gives [1, 1, 2, 3] which is correct.

Another case: [1, 2, 3, 4] - already sorted, 0 mismatches → valid.

Another case: [1, 3, 2, 4] - sorted is [1, 2, 3, 4]. Mismatches at position 1 and 2 (3 and 2). Swapping positions 1 and 2 gives [1, 2, 3, 4] → valid.

So the algorithm is:
1. Create sorted version of the array
2. Find all indices where original[i] != sorted[i]
3. If count is 0 or 2, return "YES"
4. Otherwise, return "NO"

## Edge Cases

- Duplicate values: handled naturally because if two positions have different values but should have swapped values, swapping fixes it
- All elements equal: already sorted, 0 mismatches → YES

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where elements differ
mismatch_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        mismatch_positions.append(i)

# If 0 mismatches (already sorted) or exactly 2 mismatches (can be fixed by one swap)
if len(mismatch_positions) == 0 or len(mismatch_positions) == 2:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sorted array that might have been changed by swapping two elements
2. We need to determine if the array can be sorted with at most one swap operation
3. "At most one swap" means either:
   - The array is already sorted (0 swaps needed)
   - The array can be sorted with exactly 1 swap (swap two elements)

## Analysis

Let me think about when an array can be sorted with at most one swap:

1. **Case 1: Already sorted** - No swaps needed, answer is YES
2. **Case 2: Can be sorted with exactly one swap** - This happens when there are exactly two positions where the values are incorrect

To check if we can sort with one swap:
- Find the positions where current element ≠ expected element in sorted array
- If there are 0 positions, already sorted → YES
- If there are exactly 2 positions, swap those two elements and check if it results in sorted array → YES
- If there are more than 2 positions, we need more than one swap → NO

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Create a sorted version of the array
2. Compare original array with sorted array to find positions where they differ
3. Count the number of differing positions
4. If count is 0 or 2, return YES; otherwise NO

Let me verify with examples:
- Example 1: [1,2] vs [1,2] → 0 differences → YES
- Example 2: [3,2,1] vs [1,2,3] → differences at positions 0,1,2 → 3 differences → should be NO?

Wait, let me check example 2 again. The note says we can swap elements 1 and 3, meaning swap 3 and 1 at positions 0 and 2.

Original: [3,2,1]
Swap positions 0 and 2: [1,2,3] → sorted!

So positions 0 and 2 differ: only 2 positions → YES.

For example 3: [4,3,2,1] vs [1,2,3,4]
Differences at positions 0,1,2,3 → 4 differences → more than 2 → NO.

## Algorithm

1. Create sorted version of array
2. Find all indices where original[i] != sorted[i]
3. If count of such indices is 0 or 2, return "YES"
4. Otherwise return "NO"

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where elements differ
differing_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        differing_positions.append(i)

# Check if we can sort with at most one swap
# 0 differences: already sorted (0 swaps)
# 2 differences: can be fixed with one swap
# More than 2 differences: need more than one swap
if len(differing_positions) == 0 or len(differing_positions) == 2:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sorted array that might have been modified by swapping some elements
2. We need to determine if the array can be sorted with at most one swap operation
3. A swap operation means swapping exactly two elements (not necessarily adjacent)
4. We need to output "YES" if it can be sorted with ≤ 1 swap, "NO" otherwise

## Analysis

Let me think about when an array can be sorted with at most one swap:

**Case 1: Array is already sorted**
- Need 0 swaps → answer is "YES"

**Case 2: Array can be sorted with exactly 1 swap**
- We need to find two positions where elements are swapped
- If we swap those two elements, the array becomes sorted

**Case 3: Array cannot be sorted with at most 1 swap**
- We need at least 2 swaps to sort it

## Algorithm

1. First, check if the array is already sorted. If yes, return "YES"
2. If not sorted, we need to find the positions where elements are out of place
3. For an array to be sortable with exactly one swap, there should be exactly two elements that are in the wrong positions
4. We can identify these positions by comparing the current array with the sorted version

## Step-by-step approach:

1. Create a sorted version of the array
2. Compare each position in the original array with the sorted array
3. Find all positions where original[i] != sorted[i]
4. If we have 0 mismatch positions → already sorted → YES
5. If we have exactly 2 mismatch positions → swap those two elements → YES  
6. If we have more than 2 mismatch positions → need more than 1 swap → NO

## Important consideration:

Since elements might not be distinct, we need to be careful. For example, if we have [1,1,2,2] and it's already sorted, that's fine. But if we have [1,2,1,2], we need to be careful about which positions to swap.

Actually, let me think again: for the array to be sortable with one swap, there must be exactly two positions where elements differ from the sorted array. When we swap those two elements, the array becomes sorted.

Wait, what about cases where there are more than two mismatches? For example, [1,2,3,4] might have duplicates, so the sorted version is the same.

Let me refine:

1. Create sorted version of array
2. Find positions where original[i] != sorted[i]
3. If 0 positions → already sorted → YES
4. If exactly 2 positions → swap those two → YES  
5. If more than 2 positions → need more than 1 swap → NO

But what if we have duplicates? For example, [1,2,1,2] - the sorted version is [1,1,2,2]. The mismatch positions will show where elements differ.

Actually, this approach should work because:
- If original[i] != sorted[i], it means element at position i is not where it should be
- For one swap to fix everything, there must be exactly 2 such positions
- When we swap those two positions, the array becomes sorted

## Edge case: duplicates
Even with duplicates, if elements are in wrong positions, we need to swap them.

Let's verify with examples:

Example 1: [1,2] → already sorted → 0 mismatches → YES ✓
Example 2: [3,2,1] → sorted is [1,2,3]
- positions: 0: 3 vs 1 → mismatch
- positions: 1: 2 vs 2 → match  
- positions: 2: 1 vs 3 → mismatch
- 2 mismatches → YES ✓
Example 3: [4,3,2,1] → sorted is [1,2,3,4]
- positions: 0: 4 vs 1 → mismatch
- positions: 1: 3 vs 2 → mismatch
- positions: 2: 2 vs 3 → mismatch
- positions: 3: 1 vs 4 → mismatch
- 4 mismatches → NO ✓

Perfect!

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where elements differ
mismatch_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        mismatch_positions.append(i)

# If no mismatches, array is already sorted
if len(mismatch_positions) == 0:
    print("YES")
# If exactly 2 mismatches, we can fix with one swap
elif len(mismatch_positions) == 2:
    print("YES")
# If more than 2 mismatches, we need more than one swap
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sorted array that might have been modified by swapping two elements
2. We need to determine if the array can be sorted with at most one swap operation
3. "At most one swap" means either:
   - The array is already sorted (0 swaps needed)
   - The array can be sorted by swapping exactly two elements (1 swap needed)

## Analysis

Let me think about when an array can be sorted with at most one swap:

1. **Already sorted**: If the array is already in non-decreasing order, answer is YES
2. **One swap needed**: If we can swap exactly two elements to make the array sorted

For the one-swap case, if we swap two elements, the array becomes sorted. This means:
- The array should have exactly two positions where the elements are wrong
- Swapping those two elements should make the entire array sorted

## Algorithm

1. First, check if the array is already sorted → YES
2. If not, find all positions where the array differs from the sorted version
3. If there are exactly 2 positions where values differ, swap them and check if the array becomes sorted
4. If there are 0 positions (already sorted) or exactly 2 positions with different values, answer is YES
5. If there are more than 2 positions with different values, answer is NO

Wait, let me reconsider. The array is already sorted in non-decreasing order, so we need to check if it can be sorted with one swap.

Actually, let me think differently:

1. Create the sorted version of the array
2. Find all indices where the original array differs from the sorted array
3. If there are 0 differences → already sorted → YES
4. If there are 2 differences → swap those two elements and check if it becomes sorted
5. If there are more than 2 differences → NO

But we don't actually need to swap, we just need to verify that swapping those two elements would fix everything.

For 2 differences at positions i and j:
- Original: a[i], a[j]
- Sorted: sorted_a[i], sorted_a[j]
- For the array to be fixable with one swap, we need a[i] = sorted_a[j] and a[j] = sorted_a[i]

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read input
2. Create sorted version of the array
3. Find indices where original array differs from sorted array
4. If 0 differences → YES (already sorted)
5. If exactly 2 differences → check if swapping the values at those positions makes the array sorted
6. If more than 2 differences → NO

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where original array differs from sorted array
diff_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        diff_positions.append(i)

# If no differences, array is already sorted
if len(diff_positions) == 0:
    print("YES")
# If exactly 2 differences, check if swapping them fixes the array
elif len(diff_positions) == 2:
    i, j = diff_positions[0], diff_positions[1]
    # Check if swapping a[i] and a[j] would make the array sorted
    # This happens if a[i] == sorted_a[j] and a[j] == sorted_a[i]
    if a[i] == sorted_a[j] and a[j] == sorted_a[i]:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
# If more than 2 differences, cannot be fixed by one swap
else:
    print("NO")
```

- Position 1: 3 != 2 → diff

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sorted array that might have been changed by swapping two elements
2. We need to determine if the array can be sorted with at most one swap operation
3. "At most one swap" means either 0 swaps (already sorted) or exactly 1 swap

## Key Insights

1. If the array is already sorted, answer is "YES" (0 swaps needed)
2. If we can sort the array by swapping exactly two elements, answer is "YES"
3. If we need more than one swap to sort the array, answer is "NO"

## Approach

1. First, check if the array is already sorted - if yes, return "YES"
2. If not sorted, we need to find which pairs of positions need to be swapped
3. For an array to be fixable with one swap, there should be exactly two positions where the elements are out of place

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: [1, 2] - already sorted → YES
Example 2: [3, 2, 1] - not sorted. If we swap positions 0 and 2: [1, 2, 3] → sorted → YES
Example 3: [4, 3, 2, 1] - not sorted. If we swap any two elements, we still have multiple out-of-place elements. Actually, let's check: 
- Swap 0,1: [3,4,2,1] → still not sorted
- Swap 0,2: [2,3,4,1] → not sorted  
- Swap 0,3: [1,3,2,4] → not sorted
- Swap 1,2: [4,2,3,1] → not sorted
- Swap 1,3: [4,1,2,3] → not sorted
- Swap 2,3: [4,3,1,2] → not sorted

We need to find the positions where the array differs from the sorted version.

## Algorithm

1. Create the target sorted array
2. Find all positions where current array[i] ≠ target[i]
3. If there are 0 positions → already sorted → YES
4. If there are exactly 2 positions → swap those two → YES  
5. If there are more than 2 positions → cannot be fixed with one swap → NO

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create the sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where elements are different from sorted version
diff_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        diff_positions.append(i)

# If no differences, array is already sorted (0 swaps needed)
if len(diff_positions) == 0:
    print("YES")
# If exactly 2 differences, we can swap those two positions
elif len(diff_positions) == 2:
    pos1, pos2 = diff_positions[0], diff_positions[1]
    # Verify that swapping these two positions would make the array sorted
    # That means a[pos1] should equal sorted_a[pos2] and a[pos2] should equal sorted_a[pos1]
    if a[pos1] == sorted_a[pos2] and a[pos2] == sorted_a[pos1]:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
# If more than 2 differences, cannot fix with one swap
else:
    print("NO")
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array that was originally sorted in non-decreasing order
2. Someone might have swapped some elements, and we need to determine if the array could have been changed by swapping at most one pair of elements
3. We need to check if we can sort the array with at most one swap operation

## Key Insights

1. If the array is already sorted, we need 0 swaps → YES
2. If we can sort the array with exactly 1 swap, then YES
3. If we need more than 1 swap to sort the array, then NO

## Algorithm Approach

1. First, check if the array is already sorted → YES
2. If not, we need to find if there's exactly one pair of indices (i, j) such that swapping a[i] and a[j] makes the array sorted
3. To do this efficiently:
   - Create a sorted version of the array
   - Compare the original array with the sorted array to find positions where they differ
   - If there are 0 differences → already sorted (0 swaps)
   - If there are 2 differences → we can swap these two positions (1 swap)
   - If there are more than 2 differences → we need more than 1 swap

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input array
2. Create a sorted version of the array
3. Compare original and sorted arrays to find mismatched positions
4. Count the number of positions where they differ
5. If count is 0 or 2, return "YES" (0 swaps or 1 swap)
6. If count is greater than 2, return "NO"

## Edge Cases

- Duplicates: Need to handle when there are duplicate values
- Multiple differences: More than 2 differences means we need more than one swap

Let me verify with examples:
- [1,2]: already sorted → 0 differences → YES
- [3,2,1]: sorted is [1,2,3]. Differences at positions 0,1,2 → 3 differences → NO? Wait, let me reconsider.

Actually, let me think again. For [3,2,1]:
- Position 0: 3 vs 1 → mismatch
- Position 1: 2 vs 2 → match  
- Position 2: 1 vs 3 → mismatch

So differences at positions 0 and 2 → 2 differences → can swap positions 0 and 2 → YES

For [4,3,2,1]:
- Position 0: 4 vs 1 → mismatch
- Position 1: 3 vs 2 → mismatch
- Position 2: 2 vs 3 → mismatch
- Position 3: 1 vs 4 → mismatch

4 differences → need more than 1 swap → NO

This matches the expected output.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where original and sorted arrays differ
differences = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        differences.append(i)

# If no differences, array is already sorted (0 swaps needed)
if len(differences) == 0:
    print("YES")
# If exactly 2 differences, we can swap those two elements
elif len(differences) == 2:
    # Verify that swapping these two positions would make the array sorted
    i, j = differences[0], differences[1]
    # After swap, position i should have sorted_a[j] and position j should have sorted_a[i]
    # Check if this makes the array sorted
    temp_a = a[:]
    temp_a[i], temp_a[j] = temp_a[j], temp_a[i]
    
    # Check if temp_a is sorted
    is_sorted = True
    for k in range(n - 1):
        if temp_a[k] > temp_a[k + 1]:
            is_sorted = False
            break
    
    if is_sorted:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
# If more than 2 differences, we need more than 1 swap
else:
    print("NO")
```

Wait, I need to reconsider. The condition of exactly 2 differences might not always work due to duplicates. Let me fix this:

Actually, if we have exactly 2 positions that differ, and we swap them, does it always result in a sorted array?

Let's think: if we have two positions i and j where a[i] != sorted_a[i] and a[j] != sorted_a[j], then after swapping a[i] and a[j], we should have:
- a[i] becomes sorted_a[j]
- a[j] becomes sorted_a[i]

For the array to be sorted after this swap, we need:
- At position i: a[i] = sorted_a[j] should equal sorted_a[i] (since sorted_a is sorted)
- At position j: a[j] = sorted_a[i] should equal sorted_a[j] (since sorted_a is sorted)

This means that sorted_a[i] = sorted_a[j], which contradicts the fact that a[i] != sorted_a[i] and a[j] != sorted_a[j] unless there are duplicates.

Actually, no. The issue is simpler: if we have exactly two positions that differ, swapping those two elements should result in a sorted array if and only if the values at those positions match the expected values in the sorted array.

But since we already found that at position i, a[i] != sorted_a[i], and at position j, a[j] != sorted_a[j], after swapping, we need:
- a[i] becomes a[j], and this should equal sorted_a[i]
- a[j] becomes a[i], and this should equal sorted_a[j]

So we need: a[j] == sorted_a[i] and a[i] == sorted_a[j]

This is actually equivalent to saying that the values at positions i and j should be swapped to match the sorted array.

Since the sorted array is completely determined by the multiset of values, if a[i] is not equal to sorted_a[i] and a[j] is not equal to sorted_a[j], then for the swap to work, we need:
- a[i] == sorted_a[j] and a[j] == sorted_a[i]

This is necessary and sufficient.

But in our approach, we can just check if the array becomes sorted after swapping those two positions. Given that we only have two positions that differ from the sorted array, and we're swapping exactly those two, if that results in a sorted array, then YES.

Let me simplify - we can just check if the array becomes sorted after swapping the two differing positions.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where original and sorted arrays differ
differences = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        differences.append(i)

# If no differences, array is already sorted (0 swaps needed)
if len(differences) == 0:
    print("YES")
# If exactly 2 differences, we can swap those two elements
elif len(differences) == 2:
    i, j = differences[0], differences[1]
    # Create a copy and swap elements at positions i and j
    temp_a = a[:]
    temp_a[i], temp_a[j] = temp_a[j], temp_a[i]
    
    # Check if the resulting array is sorted
    is_sorted = True
    for k in range(n - 1):
        if temp_a[k] > temp_a[k + 1]:
            is_sorted = False
            break
    
    print("YES" if is_sorted else "NO")
# If more than 2 differences, we need more than 1 swap
else:
    print("NO")
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array that was originally sorted in non-decreasing order
2. Someone might have swapped some elements (possibly two elements)
3. We need to determine if the array can be sorted with at most one swap operation
4. This means either:
   - The array is already sorted (0 swaps needed)
   - The array can be sorted with exactly one swap of two elements

## Key Insights

1. If the array is already sorted, answer is "YES"
2. If we need more than one swap to sort the array, answer is "NO"
3. In one swap operation, we swap two elements. After one swap, the array should be sorted.

## Approach

1. First, check if the array is already sorted. If yes, return "YES"
2. If not sorted, we need to find the positions where the array differs from the sorted version
3. In a properly sorted array, we can identify the positions where elements are wrong
4. For one swap to fix the array, there should be exactly 2 positions where the values are incorrect, and swapping those two elements would make the array sorted.

Let me think through this more carefully:

- If the array is already sorted → YES
- If we need more than one swap to sort it → NO  
- If we can sort it with exactly one swap → YES

To check if we can sort with exactly one swap:
1. Create the sorted version of the array
2. Find all positions where the original array differs from the sorted array
3. If there are 0 positions → already sorted → YES
4. If there are exactly 2 positions → check if swapping those two elements in the original array would produce the sorted array → YES
5. If there are more than 2 positions → cannot be fixed with one swap → NO

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, when we swap two elements, it affects only those two positions. So in the original array, we should have at most 2 positions that need to be fixed.

But also, the elements at those positions must be exactly the elements that should be at those positions in the sorted array (just swapped).

## Algorithm

1. Create a sorted copy of the array
2. Find all indices where original[i] != sorted[i]
3. If 0 indices: already sorted → YES
4. If exactly 2 indices: check if swapping these two positions in original array produces the sorted array → YES  
5. If more than 2 indices: NO

Let me trace through examples:

Example 1: [1,2] → already sorted → 0 differences → YES ✓

Example 2: [3,2,1] → sorted is [1,2,3]
- index 0: 3 vs 1 → different
- index 1: 2 vs 2 → same  
- index 2: 1 vs 3 → different
- 2 differences at indices 0 and 2
- Swap original[0] and original[2]: [1,2,3] → sorted → YES ✓

Example 3: [4,3,2,1] → sorted is [1,2,3,4]
- index 0: 4 vs 1 → different
- index 1: 3 vs 2 → different  
- index 2: 2 vs 3 → different
- index 3: 1 vs 4 → different
- 4 differences → more than 2 → NO ✓

This looks correct.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted version of the array
sorted_a = sorted(a)

# Find positions where original differs from sorted
differing_positions = []
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] != sorted_a[i]:
        differing_positions.append(i)

# If no differences, array is already sorted
if len(differing_positions) == 0:
    print("YES")
# If exactly 2 differences, check if swapping would work
elif len(differing_positions) == 2:
    i, j = differing_positions[0], differing_positions[1]
    # Swap a[i] and a[j] and check if it becomes sorted
    temp_a = a[:]
    temp_a[i], temp_a[j] = temp_a[j], temp_a[i]
    
    # Check if this produces the sorted array
    if temp_a == sorted_a:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
# If more than 2 differences, cannot be fixed with one swap
else:
    print("NO")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Output

17

1 4

The cycle edges are: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,5), (5,1)

The capital-to-all edges are:
- Capital 1: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4), (1,5)
- Capital 4: (4,1), (4,2), (4,3), (4,5)

Now taking union:
- (1,2): present (from cycle and capital)
- (1,3): new (from capital)
- (1,4): present (cycle? 1-4 is not edge, capital connection)
- (1,5): present (cycle edge)
- (2,3): present (cycle)
- (2,4): new (from capital 4)
- (3,4): present (cycle)
- (3,5): new (from capital 4? no, capital 4 has (4,3), (4,2), (4,1), (4,5))
- (4,5): present (cycle)

Let me list all unique edges:

From cycle: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,5), (5,1)

From capital connections: (1,3), (1,4), (4,2), (4,3)

(1,4) is not in cycle, (1,3) is not, (4,2) is not, (4,3) is not.

So new edges are: (1,3), (1,4), (2,4), (3,4) is in cycle, (4,3) is same as (3,4)

Unique edges:
- (1,2): has
- (1,3): new
- (1,4): new
- (1,5): has
- (2,3): has
- (2,4): new
- (3,4): has
- (4,5): has
- (5,1): same as (1,5)

So new edges: (1,3), (1,4), (2,4)

Now calculate sum of products:
Cycle edges: (1,2):3*5=15, (2,3):5*2=10, (3,4):2*2=4, (4,5):2*4=8, (5,1):4*3=12 → sum=15+10+4+8+12=49

New edges: (1,3):3*2=6, (1,4):3*2=6, (2,4):5*2=10 → sum=6+6+10=22

Yes! So the correct approach is:

1. Start with the cycle edges: sum of c[i] * c[i+1] for i=1 to n-1, and c[n] * c[1]
2. Add edges from capital cities to other cities, but only if the edge is not already in the cycle
3. For an edge (x,y) to be added, it must be a capital-to-other edge and not a cycle edge

For each capital city x, for each other city y, if (x,y) is not a cycle edge, then add c[x] * c[y]

In the cycle, the edges are only between consecutive cities.

So for each capital city x, we need to find all y such that (x,y) is not a cycle edge.

The cycle edges are between i and i+1 (mod n).

So for a capital at position x, its neighbors in the cycle are (x-1) and (x+1) mod n.

If y is one of these neighbors, then (x,y) is a cycle edge and should be skipped.

Otherwise, we add c[x] * c[y].

Also, in the capital connections, we include (x,x) which is invalid, so we skip y=x.

So algorithm:

1. Read n, k, c, capitals
2. total_cycle = sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i in 0 to n-1
3. For each capital city x (index x), total_capital_add = 0
   - For each city y (0 to n-1), if y != x and (x,y) is not a cycle edge:
     - add c[x] * c[y] to total_capital_add
4. Answer = total_cycle + total_capital_add

But is (x,y) a cycle edge? (x,y) is a cycle edge if |x-y| = 1 or |x-y| = n-1 (when considering circular).

More precisely, in a cycle of n cities, there is a direct edge between x and y if (|x-y| == 1 or (x-y) % n == 1 or (y-x) % n == 1)

Since it's a cycle, two cities x and y have a direct edge in the cycle if:
- y = (x+1) % n, or
- y = (x-1) % n

So for each capital at x, for each other city y, if y is not (x+1)%n and not (x-1)%n, then we add c[x] * c[y].

We can optimize by first calculating the sum of c for all cities, then subtracting the contributions to the neighbors.

For a capital at x, sum of c[y] for y != x is (total_sum - c[x])

We need to subtract the contributions from its two neighbors.

The contribution from neighbor at (x+1)%n is c[x] * c[(x+1)%n]
The contribution from neighbor at (x-1)%n is c[x] * c[(x-1)%n]

So total capital contribution from capital x = c[x] * (total_sum - c[x]) - c[x] * c[(x+1)%n] - c[x] * c[(x-1)%n]
= c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - c[(x+1)%n] - c[(x-1)%n])

Then total answer = total_cycle + sum over all capitals x of c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - c[(x+1)%n] - c[(x-1)%n])

Let me verify with example 1:

total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
total_cycle = c[0]*c[1] + c[1]*c[2] + c[2]*c[3] + c[3]*c[0] = 2*3 + 3*1 + 1*2 + 2*2 = 6+3+2+4 = 15

c[(2+1)%4] = c[3] = 2
c[(2-1)%4] = c[1] = 3

So algorithm:

1. total_cycle = sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i in 0 to n-1
2. total_capital_additional = 0
3. for each capital city x:
   - for each city y such that y is not in {x, (x+1)%n, (x-1)%n} and y is not a capital:

4. for each pair of capital cities (x, y) with x < y, if |x-y| != 1 and |x-y| != n-1 (not adjacent in cycle), then add c[x] * c[y]
5. answer = total_cycle + total_capital_additional

Yes.

So algorithm:

1. Read input
2. total_cycle = sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i in range(n)
3. total_additional = 0
4. capital_set = set of capital indices
5. For each capital x in capital_list:
   - for each city y from 0 to n-1:
     - if y == x: skip
     - if y in capital_set: skip  # only add to non-capitals
     - if y is adjacent to x in cycle (i.e., y == (x+1)%n or y == (x-1)%n): skip
     - else: add c[x] * c[y]
6. for each pair of capital cities (x, y) with x < y:
   - if x and y are not adjacent in cycle (i.e., not (|x-y| == 1 or (x-y) % n == 1 or (y-x) % n == 1)) then add c[x] * c[y]
7. answer = total_cycle + total_additional

But step 6 may be O(k^2), and k can be 100,000, so not efficient.

We can optimize step 6 by: the sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of capitals that are not adjacent.

Total sum over all pairs of capitals: sum_{x<y in capitals} c[x] * c[y] = (sum c[x for x in capitals]^2 - sum c[x]^2) / 2

Sum over adjacent pairs of capitals in the cycle: only when |x-y| = 1 or |x-y| = n-1 (adjacent in the cycle)

So if we can compute the sum for adjacent capital pairs, then total for non-adjacent pairs = (sum over pairs)^2 / 2 - sum over adjacent pairs

But only if the capitals are in a cycle and we need to check if they are adjacent.

In a cycle, two cities x and y are adjacent if |x-y| = 1 or |x-y| = n-1 (since in a cycle, for n>2, the only adjacent pairs are when difference is 1 or n-1).

For example in n=5, |x-y| = 1 or 4.

So for each pair of capitals, if |x-y| == 1 or |x-y| == n-1, then they are adjacent.

So sum for adjacent capital pairs = sum over pairs (x,y) with |x-y| in {1, n-1} of c[x] * c[y]

Then sum for non-adjacent pairs = ( (sum c_cap)^2 - sum c_cap^2 ) / 2 - sum_adjacent_pairs

Then add that to total_additional.

Also, in step 5, we have the capital-to-non-capital not adjacent.

We can compute that as: for each capital x, c[x] * (sum of c[y] for y in non-capitals and not in {x, (x+1)%n, (x-1)%n}))

We can precompute the sum of c for non-capitals: non_cap_sum = total_sum - sum_{cap} c[cap]

Then for each capital x, we need to subtract the values at (x+1)%n, (x-1)%n if they are non-capitals.

But we only subtract if they are non-capitals.

So for each capital x, the sum of c[y] for non-capitals and not in { (x+1)%n, (x-1)%n } is: non_cap_sum - (c[(x+1)%n] if (x+1)%n is non-capital) - (c[(x-1)%n] if (x-1)%n is non-capital)

Then the contribution for capital x is c[x] times that sum.

Then total_additional = sum over capitals of c[x] * ( that sum ) + ( (sum c_cap)^2 - sum c_cap^2 ) / 2 - sum over adjacent capital pairs of c[x] * c[y]

This is very complex, but let's try for example 2.

Example 2: capitals at 0,3, values 3,2
non_cap_sum = 16 - (3+2) = 11
 sum c_cap = 5, sum c_cap^2 = 9+4=13, (5^2 - 13)/2 = (25-13)/2 = 12/2 = 6

Adjacent pairs in the cycle: |0-3| = 3, n-1=4, so not 1 or 4, so no adjacent pair of capitals.

So sum_adjacent = 0

For capital 0: (0+1)%5=1 (c[1]=5, is non-capital), (0-1)%5=4 (c[4]=4, is non-capital)
 so subtract 5+4 = 9
 sum = 11 - 9 = 2
 contribution = 3 * 2 = 6

For capital 3: (3+1)%5=4 (c[4]=4, non-capital), (3-1)%5=2 (c[2]=2, non-capital)
 subtract 4+2 = 6
 sum = 11 - 6 = 5
 contribution = 2 * 5 = 10

Total for capital-to-non-capital = 6+10 = 16

Then for capital-capital: 6 - 0 = 6

Total additional = 16 + 6 = 22

Total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Yes! So it works.

So the steps are:

1. Read n, k, c, capitals
2. total_cycle = sum_{i} c[i] * c[(i+1)%n]
3. total_capital_val = sum of c[x] for x in capitals
4. non_cap_sum = total_sum - total_capital_val
5. sum_cap_sq = sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
6. sum_cap_pairs = ( total_capital_val ** 2 - sum_cap_sq ) // 2  # sum of c[x]*c[y] for all pairs of capitals
7. adjacent_cap_pairs_sum = 0
   - for each pair of capitals (x,y) with x < y:
     - if |x-y| == 1 or |x-y| == n-1 ( adjacent in cycle):
         - adjacent_cap_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[y]
8. for each capital x:
   - let adj1 = (x+1) % n
   - let adj2 = (x-1) % n
   - if adj1 in non_capitals and not in capitals: then value1 = c[adj1]
   - else value1 = 0
   - if adj2 in non_capitals and not in capitals: then value2 = c[adj2]
   - else value2 = 0
   - sum_non_adj = non_cap_sum - value1 - value2
   - total_additional += c[x] * sum_non_adj
9. total_capital_capital = sum_cap_pairs - adjacent_cap_pairs_sum
10. answer = total_cycle + total_additional + total_capital_capital

In step 8, we only subtract if the adjacent city is non-capital.

In step 7, we have to be careful: |x-y| might be greater than n/2, so we should use min(|x-y|, n-|x-y|) == 1 for adjacent.

In a cycle, two cities x and y are adjacent if min(|x-y|, n-|x-y|) == 1

For example in n=5, |0-3|=3, min(3,2)=2, not 1, so not adjacent.

|0-1|=1, min(1,4)=1, adjacent.

|0-4|=4, min(4,1)=1, adjacent.

So we should use if min(|x-y|, n-|x-y|) == 1

Similarly for the adjacent of a capital, we should only subtract if the city is non-capital.

So final implementation.

For each capital x, if (x+1)%n in capitals, then we have a pair (x, (x+1)%n) and we should add c[x] * c[(x+1)%n] to adjacent_cap_pairs_sum
 similarly for (x-1)%n.

Note: we must be cautious with indices. 
We are in 0-indexed.

 Steps in code:
1. n, k = map(int, input().split())
2. c = list of n integers
3. capitals = list of k integers, in 1-indexed, so convert to 0-indexed by subtracting 1
4. total_cycle = 0
   for i in range(n):
       total_cycle += c[i] * c[(i+1) % n]
5. total_capital_val = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
6. non_cap_sum = total_sum - total_capital_val
7. sum_cap_sq = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
8. sum_cap_pairs = (total_capital_val * total_capital_val - sum_cap_sq) // 2
9. adjacent_cap_pairs_sum = 0
   capital_set = set(capitals)
   for x in capitals:
       if (x+1) % n in capital_set:
           adjacent_cap_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[(x+1) % n]
       if (x-1) % n in capital_set and (x-1) % n > x:
           adjacent_cap_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[(x-1) % n]
10. for each capital x in capitals:
      adj1 = (x+1) % n
      adj2 = (x-1) % n
      value1 = 0
      value2 = 0
      if adj1 not in capital_set:  # not in capital_set means it is non-capital
          value1 = c[adj1]
      if adj2 not in capital_set:
          value2 = c[adj2]
      sum_non_adj = non_cap_sum - value1 - value2
      total_additional += c[x] * sum_non_adj
11. total_capital_capital = sum_cap_pairs - adjacent_cap_pairs_sum
12. answer = total_cycle + total_additional + total_capital_capital

Note: in step 10, we are only subtracting if the adjacent city is non-capital, and we are for each capital, adding to non-capitals that are not adjacent.

Capitals given in 1-indexed, convert to 0-indexed by subtracting 1.

Let me write the code. 
We hope it is efficient: O(n + k) for the loop over capitals and the cycle, and O(1) for each capital, and the set lookup is O(1).

The set for capital_set is built from the list of capitals (0-indexed).

Final code: 
 (We must be careful with the indices in the cycle and in the capital_set. We are using 0-indexed throughout.)


```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals_1_indexed = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals_1_indexed]

# If there are no capitals, then only the cycle edges
total_sum = sum(c)
# total_cycle: sum of c[i] * c[(i+1) % n] for i in 0 to n-1
total_cycle = 0
for i in range(n):
    total_cycle += c[i] * c[(i+1) % n]

# Calculate values for capital to non-capitals (not adjacent)
total_capital_val = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
non_cap_sum = total_sum - total_capital_val
sum_cap_sq = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)

# Sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y
sum_cap_pairs = (total_capital_val * total_capital_val - sum_cap_sq) // 2

# adjacent_cap_pairs_sum: sum of c[x] * c[y] for pairs of capitals that are adjacent in the cycle
adjacent_cap_pairs_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    # Check if (x+1) % n is in capital_set
    next_city = (x + 1) % n
    if next_city in capital_set:
        adjacent_cap_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[next_city]
    # Check if (x-1) % n is in capital_set and its index > x
    prev_city = (x - 1) % n
    if prev_city in capital_set and prev_city > x:
        adjacent_cap_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[prev_city]

# Now, for each capital x, add the product with non-capitals that are not adjacent to x
total_additional = 0
for x in capitals:
    # The adjacent cities in the cycle to x
    next_city = (x + 1) % n
    prev_city = (x - 1) % n
    # If a city is not in capital_set, then it is non-capital, so we subtract its value if it is adjacent to x
    value_next = c[next_city] if next_city not in capital_set else 0
    value_prev = c[prev_city] if prev_city not in capital_set else 0
    # The sum of c[y] for non-capitals and not adjacent to x
    sum_non_adj = non_cap_sum - value_next - value_prev
    total_additional += c[x] * sum_non_adj

# The sum of products for pairs of capitals that are not adjacent in the cycle
total_capital_capital = sum_cap_pairs - adjacent_cap_pairs_sum

answer = total_cycle + total_additional + total_capital_capital
print(answer)
```

I have now implemented the solution. It should work for the examples. 
Note: the condition "prev_city > x" ensures we only add when the previous city is greater than x, which in a cycle, for the edge between x and x-1, we only add when x-1 > x, which only happens when x=0 and x-1 = n-1 >0, and we only add once. This is correct. 

Let me test with example 1 and example 2 in mind. 
Example 1: already verified to give 17.
Example 2: should give 71.

We output the code. 
Note: in the example 2, when we have a capital at 0 and 3, and we only add when the city is in the set and index > x, for (0,4) in a cycle of 5, when x=0, (0-1)%5 =4, and 4>0 is true, so if 4 in capital_set, we add. In example 2, 4 is not in capital_set, so not added. Similarly for (3,2): when x=3, (3-1)%5=2, 2>3 is false, so not added. So only when (x+1)%n in capital_set, which in example 2, for x=0,1 in capitals, for x=0, (0+1)=1 in capital_set? 1 is not in capitals (capital at 0 and 3), so no. for x=3, (3+1)=4 not in. so adjacent_cap_pairs_sum=0, which is correct. 

So it should be correct. 
Final code. 
Note: in the condition for the previous city, we use "prev_city > x" to avoid double counting. This is safe because in a cycle, for each edge between two capitals, it will be considered only once, when the edge is in the direction of the smaller index. 

We output. 
Note: the only potential issue is that in a cycle, if the edge is between x and (x-1)%n, and (x-1)%n > x, then when we consider x, we add, and when we consider (x-1)%n, we will not add because (x-1)%n -1 might be less than (x-1)%n. But in our condition, we only add when the city is > x, so if (x-1)%n > x, then we only add when x is considered. 

And (x-1)%n > x only when (x-1) % n > x, which as we said, only when x=0 and (n-1) >0, which is true. 

So it should be only once. 

Therefore, the code is correct. 
We run it.

We are good.

 and we have not in the set for (0+1)=1, (0-1)=4, (3+1)=4, (3-1)=2. 
 only when in set and index > x. 

 in example 2, only when index > x: for x=0, 4>0, but 4 not in set; for x=3, 2>3 is false. 
 so no addition.

 (We have to be cautious with the order, but we only add once per edge.)



Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

It is easy to see that summary price is equal to 71.
