Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities. Each capital city is connected to every other city directly
3. The roads form a cyclic base structure plus additional direct connections from each capital to all other cities
4. The price of each road between cities `i` and `j` is `c_i * c_j`
5. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads

## Road Types

There are two types of roads:
1. **Cyclic roads**: 1-2, 2-3, ..., (n-1)-n, n-1 (forming a cycle)
2. **Capital-to-all roads**: Each capital city is connected directly to every other city

## Key Insight

The total price is the sum of products for all roads.

Let me break down the total sum:

**For cyclic roads**: We have roads between consecutive cities and between city n and city 1.
- Road between i and i+1 (for i from 1 to n-1): price = `c_i * c_{i+1}`
- Road between n and 1: price = `c_n * c_1`
- Total cyclic price = `sum of c_i * c_{i+1} for i=1 to n-1` + `c_n * c_1`

**For capital connections**: Each capital city is connected to every other city.
- For a capital at position x, it connects to all cities except itself
- For each capital at position x, the contribution is `c_x * (sum of c_i for all i ≠ x)`
- But we must be careful not to double count - each capital-to-other connection is unique

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem says "each capital city is connected with each other city directly", so if we have multiple capitals, each capital has its own direct connections to all other cities.

So for capital at position x, the contribution is `c_x * (sum of all c_i for i ≠ x)`.

But this counts each edge once, not multiple times. Since each capital has direct connections to all others, and we're not double counting edges (it's one edge between two cities), each edge is counted once.

## Total Solution

Total price = (cyclic roads sum) + (capital connections sum)

For capital connections:
- For each capital at position x, we add `c_x * (sum of all c_i for i ≠ x)`
- This equals `c_x * (total_sum - c_x)` where total_sum is sum of all beauty values

So capital connections sum = `sum over all capitals of (c_x * (total_sum - c_x))`
= `total_sum * (number of capitals) - sum of (c_x * c_x) for all capitals`

Total price = (cyclic roads sum) + (capital connections sum)

## Step-by-step calculation:

1. Calculate total_sum of all beauty values
2. Calculate cyclic roads sum: `c_1*c_2 + c_2*c_3 + ... + c_{n-1}*c_n + c_n*c_1`
3. Calculate capital connections sum: `k * total_sum - sum(c_x^2 for all capitals x)`
4. Add them together

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
beauties = list(map(int, input().split()))
capital_indices = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capital_indices = [x - 1 for x in capital_indices]

# Calculate total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(beauties)

# Calculate cyclic roads sum: sum of c_i * c_{i+1} for i=0 to n-2, and c_{n-1} * c_0
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cyclic_sum += beauties[i] * beauties[j]

# Calculate capital connections sum
capital_sq_sum = 0
for cap_idx in capital_indices:
    capital_sq_sum += beauties[cap_idx] * beauties[cap_idx]

# Each capital connects to all other cities
# For a capital at position x, contribution is c_x * (total_sum - c_x)
# Sum over all capitals: k * total_sum - sum(c_x^2)
capital_connections_sum = k * total_sum - capital_sq_sum

# Total price is sum of cyclic roads and capital connections
total_price = cyclic_sum + capital_connections_sum

print(total_price)
```

capital_sq_sum = 0
for cap_idx in capital_indices:
    capital_sq_sum += beauties[cap_idx] * beauties[cap_idx]

# For each capital, it connects to all other cities, contributing c_x * (sum of c_i for i≠x)
# Total contribution from all capital connections: k * total_sum - capital_sq_sum
capital_connections_sum = k * total_sum - capital_sq_sum

# Total price is sum of cyclic roads and capital connections
total_price = cyclic_sum + capital_connections_sum

print(total_price)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

Let me verify with example 2:

- Cyclic sum = 49
- First sum (capital to non-adjacent): 
  - Capital 1: to 3,4 → 3*2 + 3*2 = 6+6 = 12
  - Capital 4: to 1,2 → 2*3 + 2*5 = 6+10 = 16
  - Total = 12+16 = 28
- Pairs of capitals not adjacent: only (1,4) → price = 3*2 = 6
- Total = 49 + 28 - 6 = 71 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate total_cyclic = sum of c[i] * c[i+1] for i from 0 to n-2, plus c[n-1] * c[0]
2. Calculate capital_sum = 0
   - For each capital city i (1-based), find all j not in {i-1, i, i+1} (with wraparound)
   - For each such j, add c[i-1] * c[j-1] to capital_sum (0-based indexing)
3. Calculate pair_sum = 0
   - For each pair of capital cities (i,j) with i < j, if they are not adjacent in cycle, add c[i] * c[j]
4. Result = total_cyclic + capital_sum - pair_sum

But note: when we go through each capital, we are adding edges from capital to non-adjacent cities. This includes edges to other capital cities that are not adjacent.

But we are double-counting these edges between capitals.

So we need to subtract the price of each such edge once.

Now, let me implement this.

We need to be careful with indexing.

Let me code it step by step.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Step 1: Calculate sum of cyclic edges
total_cyclic = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    total_cyclic += c[i] * c[j]

# Step 2: Calculate capital_sum - sum of c[i]*c[j] for each capital i, each j not adjacent and not i
capital_sum = 0
for cap in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities in 0-based indexing
    # adjacent to cap: (cap-1) % n and (cap+1) % n
    adj1 = (cap - 1) % n
    adj2 = (cap + 1) % n
    
    # All cities except cap, adj1, adj2
    for j in range(n):
        if j == cap or j == adj1 or j == adj2:
            continue
        capital_sum += c[cap] * c[j]

# Step 3: Calculate pair_sum - sum of c[i]*c[j] for each pair of capitals (i,j) not adjacent in cycle
pair_sum = 0
cap_set = set(capitals)
# We need to check pairs of capital cities that are not adjacent
# Convert to list to make it easier
cap_list = list(cap_set)
for i in range(len(cap_list)):
    for j in range(i + 1, len(cap_list)):
        a, b = cap_list[i], cap_list[j]
        # Check if they are adjacent in cycle
        # adjacent if (a+1) % n == b or (a-1) % n == b
        adj1 = (a + 1) % n
        adj2 = (a - 1) % n
        if b == adj1 or b == adj2:
            # adjacent, skip
            continue
        # not adjacent, add price
        pair_sum += c[a] * c[b]

# Final result
result = total_cyclic + capital_sum - pair_sum
print(result)
```

Wait, but in the capital_sum, when we add edges between two capitals that are not adjacent, we are adding them twice (once from each capital). 

In our pair_sum, we are counting each such edge once.

So when we do capital_sum - pair_sum, we are effectively removing one instance of each edge between two non-adjacent capitals.

This should work.

Let me verify with example 1:
n=4, k=1, capitals=[3] (0-based: index 2), c=[2,3,1,2]

cyclic sum: 
- (0,1): 2*3=6
- (1,2): 3*1=3
- (2,3): 1*2=2
- (3,0): 2*2=4
Sum = 15

capital_sum: for cap=2
- adj1 = (2-1)%4 = 1, adj2 = (2+1)%4 = 3
- j from 0,1,2,3: skip j=2,1,3 → only j=0
- add c[2]*c[0] = 1*2 = 2

capital_sum = 2

pair_sum: only one capital, so no pairs → 0

result = 15 + 2 - 0 = 17 ✓

Example 2: we already verified it gives 71.

But the above code might be inefficient for large n and many capitals.

In worst case, if k is large, we have O(k^2) pairs, and k can be up to 100,000? No, k ≤ n ≤ 100,000, but if k is large, O(k^2) might be too slow.

We need to optimize.

Note: k can be up to 100,000, so k^2 can be 10^10, which is too slow in Python.

We need a more efficient approach.

Alternative approach:

Instead of computing the capital_sum and then subtracting the pair_sum, can we avoid the O(k^2)?

Actually, in the capital_sum, we are adding c[i] * c[j] for each capital i and each city j not adjacent to i.

This can be written as:
capital_sum = sum over each capital i of (c[i] * (sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i))

The sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i can be computed as (total_sum - c[i] - c[adj1] - c[adj2])

So capital_sum = sum over i in capitals of c[i] * (total_sum - c[i] - c[adj1] - c[adj2])

We can compute this without loops over all j.

For pair_sum, we need sum over pairs of capitals (i,j) with i < j and not adjacent.

We can compute this without nested loops by iterating through all pairs, but if k is large, this is still O(k^2).

Is there a way to avoid O(k^2)?

Perhaps we can compute the sum over all pairs of capitals of c[i]*c[j] and then subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs.

The total sum of products for all pairs of capitals = (sum of c[i] for i in capitals) ^ 2 - sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals) / 2

Then, from this, we subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs.

But we only want non-adjacent pairs.

So pair_sum = (sum_c)^2 - sum_sq - sum_adjacent_pairs

Where sum_c = sum of c[i] for i in capitals
sum_sq = sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals
sum_adjacent_pairs = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals.

But wait, we only want non-adjacent pairs that are not adjacent in cycle.

And we need to compute sum_adjacent_pairs.

We can compute this by checking for each capital, if its adjacent cities are also capitals (in the capital list), and if so, add c[i] * c[adj] for each such adjacent.

For each capital i, its adjacent cities are (i-1) and (i+1). If either is also a capital, then we have an edge between i and that adjacent capital.

So sum_adjacent_pairs = sum over each capital i of c[i] * c[adj] if adj is a capital, and we only count each edge once.

Since each edge between two capitals is counted once in the adjacency, we can do:

sum_adjacent_pairs = 0
for each capital i:
    adj1 = (i-1) % n
    adj2 = (i+1) % n
    if adj1 in capitals_set:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[i] * c[adj1]
    if adj2 in capitals_set:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[i] * c[adj2]

But this counts each edge twice? No, because if edge between i and j where j is adjacent to i, and j is a capital, we add c[i]*c[j] when processing i, and also when processing j, when j's adjacent includes i.

So we double-count.

For example, edge between i and j, when i's adjacent includes j, we add c[i]*c[j], and when j's adjacent includes i, we add c[j]*c[i], so we add 2 * c[i]*c[j].

So we need to divide by 2.

Therefore, sum_adjacent_pairs = 0
for each capital i:
    adj1 = (i-1) % n
    adj2 = (i+1) % n
    if adj1 in capitals_set and adj1 != i:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[i] * c[adj1]
    if adj2 in capitals_set and adj2 != i:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[i] * c[adj2]

Then divide by 2.

But wait, this still counts each edge twice.

For edge between i and j, we add it when i is processed (if j is adjacent to i), and when j is processed (if i is adjacent to j).

So we double-count.

Therefore, we should only add it once.

We can do: iterate over each capital i, and for each adjacent city, add c[i]*c[adj] only if adj is a capital and we haven't added it.

Better: use a set of capital indices, and for each capital i, if its adjacent city is a capital, add c[i]*c[adj] only once.

But since the edge is undirected, we can do:

sum_adjacent_pairs = 0
for each capital i:
    adj1 = (i-1) % n
    adj2 = (i+1) % n
    if adj1 in capitals_set and adj1 != i:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[i] * c[adj1]
    if adj2 in capitals_set and adj2 != i:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[i] * c[adj2]

Then divide by 2 because each edge is counted twice.

But if i and adj are both capitals and adjacent, we add c[i]*c[adj] once from i, and once from adj (when processing adj, if i is adjacent to adj), so total 2 * c[i]*c[adj].

So sum_adjacent_pairs will be twice the actual adjacent pair sum.

Therefore, actual_pair_adjacent = sum_adjacent_pairs / 2

Then, the sum of products for all pairs of capitals = (sum_c)^2 - sum_sq) / 2

Then, pair_sum = (sum_c^2 - sum_sq) / 2 - sum_adjacent_pairs / 2

But we only want non-adjacent pairs, so pair_sum = (sum_c^2 - sum_sq - sum_adjacent_pairs) / 2

And this is exactly what we need.

Now, let me verify with example 2:
capitals = [0,3] (0-based: city 1 and city 4)

c = [3,5,2,2,4]
sum_c = 3+2 = 5
sum_sq = 3^2 + 2^2 = 9 + 4 = 13
(sum_c^2 - sum_sq) / 2 = (25 - 13) / 2 = 12 / 2 = 6

sum_adjacent_pairs: 
- for i=0: adj1 = 4 (index 4), adj2 = 1
  - 4 is not in capitals (capitals={0,3}), 1 is not in capitals → add 0
- for i=3: adj1 = 2, adj2 = 4
  - 2 not in capitals, 4 not in capitals → add 0
So sum_adjacent_pairs = 0
pair_sum = (12 - 0) / 2 = 6

But in example 2, capitals are at 0 and 3 (0-based: city 1 and city 4), are they adjacent?
City 1 (0) adjacent to 4 (index 4) and 1 (index 1)
City 4 (3) adjacent to 2 and 4
So no shared adjacency. So pair_sum should be 6.

Wait, earlier we said the edge between 1 and 4 should be counted, but in 0-based indexing, city 1 is index 0, city 4 is index 3.

Are 0 and 3 adjacent in cycle 0-1-2-3-4-0?
0 adjacent to 4 and 1
3 adjacent to 2 and 4
So 0 and 3 are not adjacent.

So pair_sum = 6, which is c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6.

Yes.

In the full calculation, we have:
total_cyclic = 49
capital_sum = (c[0] * (sum of c[j] for j not adj to 0) + c[3] * (sum of c[j] for j not adj to 3))

For cap=0: adj1=4, adj2=1 → not in capitals, so sum of c[j] for j not adj to 0 = (c[0]+c[1]+c[2]+c[3]+c[4]) - c[0] - c[4] - c[1] = (3+5+2+2+4) - 3 - 4 - 5 = 16 - 12 = 4
So c[0] * 4 = 3*4 = 12

For cap=3: adj1=2, adj2=4 → not in capitals, so sum = total_sum - c[3] - c[2] - c[4] = 16 - 2 - 2 - 4 = 8
c[3] * 8 = 2*8 = 16

capital_sum = 12 + 16 = 28

pair_sum = 6

result = 49 + 28 - 6 = 71 ✓

Perfect.

Now, the complexity:
- O(n) for cyclic edges
- O(1) per capital for capital_sum (with precomputed total_sum)
- O(k) for sum_adjacent_pairs (with set lookup)
- O(k) for computing sum and sum_sq

So overall O(n + k), which is acceptable for n,k ≤ 100,000.

Let me implement.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Precompute total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Step 1: Calculate sum of cyclic edges
total_cyclic = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    total_cyclic += c[i] * c[j]

# Step 2: Calculate capital_sum
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
# For each capital, add c[i] * (sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i)
for cap in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in 0-based indexing
    adj1 = (cap - 1) % n
    adj2 = (cap + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of c[j] for j not equal to cap, adj1, adj2
    sum_adjacent = total_sum - c[cap] - c[adj1] - c[adj2]
    capital_sum += c[cap] * sum_adjacent

# Step 3: Calculate pair_sum - sum of c[i]*c[j] for each pair of capitals not adjacent in cycle
# First, compute sum of all pairs of capitals: (sum_c)^2 - sum_sq) / 2
sum_c = sum(c[cap] for cap in capitals)
sum_sq = sum(c[cap] * c[cap] for cap in capitals)
all_pairs_sum = (sum_c * sum_c - sum_sq) // 2

# Now subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals
sum_adjacent_pairs = 0
for cap in capitals:
    adj1 = (cap - 1) % n
    adj2 = (cap + 1) % n
    if adj1 in capital_set and adj1 != cap:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[cap] * c[adj1]
    if adj2 in capital_set and adj2 != cap:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[cap] * c[adj2]

# Since each adjacent edge is counted twice in sum_adjacent_pairs (once from each end), divide by 2
sum_adjacent_pairs //= 2

# The sum of products for non-adjacent pairs of capitals
pair_sum = all_pairs_sum - sum_adjacent_pairs

# Final result
result = total_cyclic + capital_sum - pair_sum
print(result)
```

Wait, in the capital_sum, when we do `sum_adjacent = total_sum - c[cap] - c[adj1] - c[adj2]`, this is correct, because we remove cap itself, and its two adjacent cities.

Yes.

And in the pair_sum, we compute all pairs of capitals, then subtract the adjacent ones.

This should be correct.

Let me test with example 1:
n=4, k=1, capitals=[2] (0-based), c=[2,3,1,2]

total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
cyclic: (0,1)=6, (1,2)=3, (2,3)=2, (3,0)=4 → sum=15

capital_sum: for cap=2
adj1 = (2-1)%4=1, adj2=(2+1)%4=3
sum_adjacent = 8 - c[2] - c[1] - c[3] = 8 - 1 - 3 - 2 = 2
capital_sum = c[2] * 2 = 1*2 = 2

sum_c = c[2] = 1
sum_sq = 1
all_pairs_sum = (1*1 - 1) // 2 = 0
sum_adjacent_pairs: for cap=2, adj1=1 (not in capitals), adj2=3 (not in capitals) → 0
pair_sum = 0

result = 15 + 2 - 0 = 17 ✓

Perfect.

I think this is correct.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Precompute total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Step 1: Calculate sum of cyclic edges
total_cyclic = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    total_cyclic += c[i] * c[j]

# Step 2: Calculate capital_sum
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
for cap in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in 0-based indexing
    adj1 = (cap - 1) % n
    adj2 = (cap + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of c[j] for j not equal to cap, adj1, adj2
    sum_adjacent = total_sum - c[cap] - c[adj1] - c[adj2]
    capital_sum += c[cap] * sum_adjacent

# Step 3: Calculate pair_sum - sum of c[i]*c[j] for each pair of capitals not adjacent in cycle
# First, compute sum of all pairs of capitals: (sum_c)^2 - sum_sq) / 2
sum_c = sum(c[cap] for cap in capitals)
sum_sq = sum(c[cap] * c[cap] for cap in capitals)
all_pairs_sum = (sum_c * sum_c - sum_sq) // 2

# Now compute sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals (each edge counted once)
sum_adjacent_pairs = 0
for cap in capitals:
    adj1 = (cap - 1) % n
    adj2 = (cap + 1) % n
    if adj1 in capital_set and adj1 != cap:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[cap] * c[adj1]
    if adj2 in capital_set and adj2 != cap:
        sum_adjacent_pairs += c[cap] * c[adj2]

# Since each adjacent edge is counted twice in the above loop (once for each endpoint), divide by 2
sum_adjacent_pairs //= 2

# The sum of products for non-adjacent pairs of capitals
pair_sum = all_pairs_sum - sum_adjacent_pairs

# Final result
result = total_cyclic + capital_sum - pair_sum
print(result)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-3-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities. Each capital city is connected to every other city directly.
3. The price of passing a road between cities i and j is `c_i * c_j`.
4. We need to find the sum of products for all roads in the country.

## Road Types

There are two types of roads:
1. **Cycle edges**: Between consecutive cities (1-2, 2-3, ..., n-1)
2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city connects to every other city

## Analysis

Let's break down the total price:

### 1. Cycle edges
For consecutive cities i and i+1 (where i from 1 to n-1), and between n and 1:
- Price = sum of `c_i * c_{i+1}` for i from 1 to n-1, plus `c_n * c_1`

### 2. Capital connections
For each capital city x, it connects to every other city except itself.
- For capital city x, the price is sum of `c_x * c_j` for all j ≠ x
- This can be written as: `c_x * (sum of all c_j - c_x) = c_x * (total_sum - c_x)`

However, note that if we have multiple capital cities, we need to be careful about double counting.

1. The n edges of the cycle
2. Additional edges from each capital city to every other city (except itself)

But the cycle already has edges between consecutive cities. The capital connections add extra edges.

So we need to avoid adding the edges that are already in the cycle.

Another thought: maybe "each capital city is connected with each other city" means that for every pair of cities, if one is capital, they are connected, regardless of the cycle.

In my previous calculation, I only added edges from each capital to non-adjacent non-capital cities, but I missed the edge between two capitals.

In my previous calculation, for capital 1, I only added to 3,4 (city 4), and for capital 4, I only added to 1,2.

For capital 1: added to 3,4 (city 4)

So edge (1,4) is added twice: once from capital 1 to 4, once from capital 4 to 1.

This is a problem.

I see! I double-counted the edge (1,4) because when capital 1 connects to city 4, and when capital 4 connects to city 1, it's the same edge.

So I need to avoid double counting.

The set of edges from capital to other cities should be considered as a set of unordered pairs.

So for each capital city, I should only add edges to non-adjacent cities, and since each edge is shared between two capitals, I need to be careful.

But the edge between two capitals only exists if they are not adjacent, and it should be added once.

In the problem, "each capital city is connected with each other city" - this means for each capital city x, it is connected to every other city y, including other capitals.

So the edge (x,y) exists for every x capital, y not x.

But if (x,y) is already in the cycle (i.e., x and y are adjacent in the cycle), then it's already there.

Otherwise, it's an additional road.

And since the edge is between two cities, we should only count it once in the total sum.

So for each pair (x,y) where x is capital and y is not x, and (x,y) is not in the cycle, we add c_x * c_y.

And if (x,y) is in the cycle, we don't add it (because it's already in the cycle).

And also, if x and y are both capital and not adjacent, we add the edge once.

This is equivalent to: for each capital city x, we add c_x * (sum of c_y for all y not x and not adjacent to x) 

But in this, for a pair (x,y) where both are capital and not adjacent, it will be added when we process x and also when we process y, so double-counted.

So to avoid double-counting, we should only add such edges once.

We can do it in two ways:

1. For each capital city x, add edges to non-adjacent non-capital cities, and edges to non-adjacent capital cities only once.

2. Add all edges from capital to non-adjacent cities, but only once per pair.

The cleanest way is to iterate over all pairs (x,y) where x is capital and y is not x and (x,y) is not in the cycle, and add c_x * c_y.

But since there are up to 100,000 cities and k up to 100,000, and we have to check for each pair whether (x,y) is in the cycle, it might be too slow.

We can do it efficiently:

- The edges in the cycle are only between consecutive cities and between last and first.

- For a capital city x, the only cities it is adjacent to in the cycle are its two neighbors.

- So for x, the non-adjacent cities are all cities except x and its two neighbors.

- Among these, some are capital, some are not.

- The edges to non-capital cities should be added (only once, but since we are going to add from each capital, we need to avoid double-counting).

Actually, if we add for each capital x, the edges to non-adjacent non-capital cities, then each such edge is added exactly once (because only one of the two endpoints is capital if the other is not capital).

For an edge between a capital and a non-capital, only one endpoint is capital, so when we process that capital, we will add the edge.

For an edge between two capitals, if they are not adjacent in the cycle, then when we process either capital, we will add the edge to the other capital, but it will be added twice.

So to avoid that, we can:

- For each capital city x, add the product c_x * c_y for all y not x and not adjacent to x and y is not a capital.

- For each pair of capitals (x,y) that are not adjacent in the cycle, add the product c_x * c_y once.

But then we might have to iterate over all pairs of capitals.

Number of capitals k can be up to 100,000, so pairs can be up to 10^10, too many.

We need a more efficient way.

We can do:

Total capital edge sum = sum_{x in capitals} c_x * (sum of c_y for all y not x and not adjacent to x)

But this double-counts the edges between two capitals that are not adjacent.

In this sum, for each edge (x,y) with both x,y in capitals and not adjacent, it is added twice: once from x to y, once from y to x.

For edges between capital and non-capital, it is added once.

So we can adjust by subtracting the double-counted parts.

Let D be the sum of c_x * c_y for all pairs (x,y) of capitals that are not adjacent in the cycle.

Then the total capital edge contribution is:
 sum_{x in capitals} c_x * (sum_{y not in {x, neighbors}} c_y) - D

Because each edge between two capitals is counted twice in the first sum, and should be counted once, so we subtract once.

But in the first sum, for a pair (x,y) both capital and not adjacent, it is included in both x and y, so twice.

We want it once, so we should subtract one copy.

So total capital edge contribution = (sum over all capital x of c_x * sum over all non-adjacent y of c_y) - (sum over pairs of non-adjacent capital-capital edges of c_x * c_y)

Now, the first part can be computed for each capital.

The second part is the sum over all pairs of capital cities that are not adjacent in the cycle of c_x * c_y.

We can compute this efficiently.

But let's see if in the example this works.

Example 2: capitals at 1 and 4 (1-indexed)

First, for each capital, sum of c_y for y not x and not adjacent.

For capital 1 (index 0):
- adjacent: 5 and 2 (cities 5,2) → indices 4,1
- not x and not adjacent: city 3,4 → indices 2,3
- sum = c[2] + c[3] = 2+2 = 4
- contribution = c[0] * 4 = 3*4 = 12

For capital 4 (index 3):
- adjacent: 3 and 5 (cities 3,5) → indices 2,4
- not x and not adjacent: city 1,2 → indices 0,1
- sum = c[0] + c[1] = 3+5 = 8
- contribution = c[3] * 8 = 2*8 = 16

Sum of both = 12+16 = 28

Now, pairs of capitals that are not adjacent: only pair (1,4)
- are they adjacent? In cycle 1-2-3-4-5-1, 1 and 4 are not adjacent (not next to each other)
- so we add c[0] * c[3] = 3*2 = 6

So we need to subtract 6.

Total capital edge contribution = 28 - 6 = 22

Cycle edges: 49

Total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Yes! It matches.

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate cycle edge sum
2. For each capital city x, calculate sum of c_y for all y not x and not adjacent to x, and add c_x * that sum
3. Calculate the sum of c_x * c_y for all pairs of capital cities (x,y) that are not adjacent in the cycle
4. Subtract that sum from step 2 to avoid double counting
5. Total = cycle edges + step 2 - step 3

In step 2, we are including both capital-capital and capital-non-capital edges, but for capital-capital edges that are not adjacent, they are included twice in step 2, so we subtract once to make them once.

For capital-non-capital edges, they are included once in step 2, and not in step 3, so correct.

For capital-capital edges that are adjacent, they are not in step 2 because they are in the cycle, so not added, and not in step 3 because we only consider non-adjacent pairs.

Perfect.

Now, how to compute step 3: sum of c_x * c_y for all pairs of capital cities that are not adjacent in the cycle.

The capitals are given as indices. We can make a list of capital indices.

For each pair of capital cities (i,j) with i < j, if they are not adjacent in the cycle, add c_i * c_j.

Two cities i and j are adjacent in the cycle if |i - j| = 1 or |i - j| = n-1 (for wrap-around).

In 1-indexed, for cities i and j, they are adjacent if:
- |i - j| = 1, or
- |i - j| = n-1 (when i=1,j=n or i=n,j=1)

In 0-indexed, for indices i and j, they are adjacent if:
- |i - j| = 1, or
- (i == 0 and j == n-1) or (i == n-1 and j == 0)

So we can do:

- total_capital_pair_sum = 0
- for each pair (i,j) of capitals with i < j:
  - if they are not adjacent, add c_i * c_j

But k can be up to 100,000, so pairs can be O(k^2), which is too many.

We need a more efficient way.

We can compute the sum of all products for pairs of capitals: (sum of c_i for i in capitals) * (sum of c_j for j in capitals) - sum of c_i^2 for i in capitals

Let S = sum of beauty values of capitals
Let T = sum of c_i^2 for i in capitals

Then total pairwise sum = S*S - T

Now, the adjacent pairs of capitals: for each adjacent pair in the cycle, if both are capitals, we need to subtract their product.

So if we have a pair of consecutive capitals in the cycle, we need to subtract c_i * c_j.

Also, the pair (0, n-1) if both are capitals.

So the sum of products for non-adjacent capital-capital pairs = (S*S - T) - (sum of c_i * c_j for adjacent capital pairs)

Then we can use this.

So steps for step 3:

- Let cap_list = list of capital indices (0-indexed)
- Let S = sum of c[x] for x in cap_list
- Let T = sum of c[x]^2 for x in cap_list
- Let adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- Check for each edge in the cycle between consecutive cities (i, (i+1) % n) whether both are capitals.
- For each such edge, if both are capitals, add c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] to adjacent_pair_sum
- Also, for edge between 0 and n-1, if both are capitals, add c[0] * c[n-1]

Then step 3 = (S*S - T) - adjacent_pair_sum

Then total capital edge contribution = (sum over capital x of c_x * (sum of c_y for y not x and not adjacent to x)) - ((S*S - T) - adjacent_pair_sum)

And total price = cycle_edges + total_capital_edge_contribution

Now, how to compute for each capital x: the sum of c_y for y not x and not adjacent to x.

This can be computed as:

total_sum - c_x - c[prev] - c[next] for each capital x

Where prev and next are the adjacent in the cycle.

So for each capital x, contribution = c_x * (total_sum - c_x - c[prev] - c[next])

Then sum over all capital x of this.

Let total_sum = sum of all c_i

Then sum_capital_contribution = sum_{x in capitals} c_x * (total_sum - c_x - c[prev] - c[next])

Then total_capital_edge_contribution = this sum - ( (S*S - T) - adjacent_pair_sum )

Let me verify with example 2:

c = [3,5,2,2,4], n=5, capitals at 0,3 (0-indexed)

total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16

For capital at 0 (city 1):
- adjacent: (0-2)%5+1 = ( -2%5 )+1 = 3+1 = 4 → index 4, c[4]=4
- next = (0%5)+1 = 1 → index 1, c[1]=5
- so exclude: city 0,4,1
- sum for y = 16 - c[0] - c[4] - c[1] = 16 - 3 - 4 - 5 = 4
- contribution = c[0] * 4 = 3*4 = 12

For capital at 3 (city 4):
- adjacent: (3-2)%5+1 = 1+1 = 2 → index 2, c[2]=2
- next = (3%5)+1 = 3+1 = 4 → index 4, c[4]=4
- exclude: city 3,2,4
- sum = 16 - c[3] - c[2] - c[4] = 16 - 2 - 2 - 4 = 8
- contribution = 2*8 = 16

Sum = 12+16 = 28

Now, S = 3+2 = 5
T = 3^2 + 2^2 = 9+4 = 13
S*S - T = 25-13 = 12

adjacent_pair_sum: check edges
- (0,1): city 1 and 2: city 1 is capital, city 2 is not (c[1]=5) → not both capital
- (1,2): 2: not, 3: not
- (2,3): 3: is capital, 4: is capital? city 4 is index 3, city 3 is index 2? city at index 2 is city 3, index 3 is city 4. So cities 3 and 4: city 3 is not capital (capital at 1 and 4), city 4 is capital. So only one capital.
- (3,4): city 4 and 5: city 4 is capital, city 5 is not (index 4: c[4]=4, not capital)
- (4,0): city 5 and 1: city 5 not, city 1 is capital → only one.

So no adjacent pair of capitals, so adjacent_pair_sum = 0

Then step 3 = 12 - 0 = 12

Then total_capital_edge_contribution = 28 - 12 = 16? Wait, should be 22.

I see: step 3 should be the sum of c_x * c_y for non-adjacent capital-capital pairs, which is 12 in this case (only pair (0,3) with values 3 and 2, 3*2=6), but I have 12.

The sum of all pairwise products is 12, including the adjacent ones.

We have only one pair: (0,3) with values 3 and 2, product 6.

Is (0,3) adjacent? In cycle: indices 0 and 3, |0-3|=3, not 1 or 4 (n-1=4), so not adjacent.

So non-adjacent pair sum should be 6.

But I calculated S*S - T = 5*5 - (9+4) = 25-13 = 12, which includes the pair (0,3) with 6, and other pairs? Only one pair of capitals.

Capitals: only two, so only one pair: (0,3) with product 6.

So S*S - T should be 6, not 12.

S = 3+2 = 5, T = 9+4 = 13, 25-13=12, but 3*2=6, so 12 is not 6.

I see the mistake: S*S - T = (c0 + c3)^2 - (c0^2 + c3^2) = 2*c0*c3 = 2*3*2 = 12

Yes! Because (a+b)^2 - (a^2+b^2) = 2ab.

So for two capitals, it is 2*c0*c3 = 2*6 = 12.

But we want the sum of products for all pairs, which for two capitals is only one pair: c0*c3 = 6.

So we have double-counted the pair.

In general, for a set of capital values, the sum over all unordered pairs of c_i * c_j is (sum c_i)^2 - sum c_i^2) / 2

Yes! Because (a+b+c+..)^2 = sum a^2 + 2*sum_{i<j} a_i a_j

So sum_{i<j} a_i a_j = (S^2 - T) / 2

In this case, (25 - 13) / 2 = 12/2 = 6 ✓

Then the sum of c_x * c_y for non-adjacent capital-capital pairs = (S^2 - T) / 2 - (adjacent_pair_sum)

Where adjacent_pair_sum is the sum of c_i * c_j for each adjacent pair of capitals.

In example, adjacent_pair_sum = 0, so non-adjacent = 6 - 0 = 6

Then total_capital_edge_contribution = 28 - 6 = 22

Yes! This matches.

So the algorithm is:

1. Read n, k, beauty values c, and capital indices (1-indexed)
2. Convert capital indices to 0-indexed
3. total_sum = sum(c)
4. cycle_edges = 0
   for i in range(n):
       j = (i+1) % n
       cycle_edges += c[i] * c[j]
5. For each capital city x, compute:
   - prev_city = (x-2) % n + 1  → index: (x-2) % n + 1 is not correct for 0-indexed
   In 0-indexed: for city x (0-indexed), adjacent are:
   - prev = (x-2) % n + 1? Let's use: for city x, indices:
   - prev = (x-1) % n  (0-indexed, (x-1) % n)
   - next = (x+1) % n
   So for x, adjacent indices are (x-1) % n and (x+1) % n
   So for capital x, sum of c_y for y not x and not adjacent = total_sum - c[x] - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n]
   contribution = c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n])
   sum_capital_contribution += contribution

6. Let S = sum of c[x] for x in capitals
7. Let T = sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
8. adjacent_pair_sum = 0
   for i in range(n):
        j = (i+1) % n
        if i in capitals and j in capitals:
           adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]
9. total_pair_sum = (S*S - T) // 2   # sum over all unordered pairs of capitals
10. capital_edge_sum = sum_capital_contribution - (total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum)
11. total_price = cycle_edges + capital_edge_sum

In step 8, we are iterating over edges and checking if both endpoints are in capitals.

But in step 9, we have to be careful: we want the sum over unordered pairs that are adjacent in the cycle, which is exactly what we are doing by iterating over edges.

Since each edge is between two cities, and we are iterating over each edge once, and if both are capitals, we add the product.

In a cycle, each edge is between two consecutive cities, so we cover each adjacent pair once.

Perfect.

Let me verify with example 1:

n=4, c=[2,3,1,2], capitals=[3] (1-indexed) → 0-indexed index 2

total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8

cycle_edges:
0-1: 2*3=6
1-2:3*1=3
2-3:1*2=2
3-0:2*2=4
sum = 15

For capital at 2 (0-indexed):
- prev = (2-1)%4 = 1, c[1]=3
- next = (2+1)%4 = 3, c[3]=2
- sum = 8 - c[2] - c[1] - c[3] = 8 - 1 - 3 - 2 = 2
- contribution = 1 * 2 = 2

sum_capital_contribution = 2

S = 1, T = 1, (1-1)//2 = 0
adjacent_pair_sum: no two capitals (only one), so 0
capital_edge_sum = 2 - (0 - 0) = 2
total_price = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

Perfect.

Now example 2: we already know it should be 71.

So I'll implement accordingly.

Edge case: when k=1, then no adjacent pairs between capitals, and only one capital, so (S*S-T)//2 = 0, and adjacent_pair_sum=0, so capital_edge_sum = sum_capital_contribution - 0.

In example 2, sum_capital_contribution = 28, (S*S-T)//2 = 6, adjacent_pair_sum=0, so capital_edge_sum = 28 - (6 - 0) = 22, total=49+22=71.

Yes.

Code accordingly. 

Note: in step 8, when iterating over edges, we need to check if both i and (i+1)%n are in the set of capital indices.

We can use a set for capitals.

Let me write the code.

Note: the capital indices are given in 1-indexed, so we convert to 0-indexed by subtracting 1.

Also, for the edge between i and (i+1)%n, we check if both i and (i+1)%n are in the capital set.

Implementation:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capital_indices = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capital_set = set()
for idx in capital_indices:
    capital_set.add(idx - 1)  # convert to 0-indexed

total_sum = sum(c)

# Compute cycle edges
cycle_edges = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_edges += c[i] * c[j]

# Compute sum of contributions from each capital to non-adjacent cities
sum_capital_contribution = 0
for x in capital_set:
    # adjacent in the cycle: (x-1) % n and (x+1) % n
    prev_idx = (x - 1) % n
    next_idx = (x + 1) % n
    # sum of c_y for y not x and not adjacent to x
    other_sum = total_sum - c[x] - c[prev_idx] - c[next_idx]
    contribution = c[x] * other_sum
    sum_capital_contribution += contribution

# Compute S and T for capitals
S = sum(c[x] for x in capital_set)
T = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capital_set)

# Compute sum of c_i * c_j for adjacent pairs of capitals in the cycle
adjacent_pair_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i in capital_set and j in capital_set:
        adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Total sum of products for all unordered pairs of capitals
total_pair_sum = (S * S - T) // 2

# The sum of products for non-adjacent capital-capital pairs
non_adjacent_capital_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

# The total contribution from capital-to-other edges (after correcting for double counting)
capital_edge_sum = sum_capital_contribution - non_adjacent_capital_pair_sum

total_price = cycle_edges + capital_edge_sum
print(total_price)
```

Let me test with the examples.

Note: in the sum of c_i * c_j for adjacent pairs, we are only including edges that are in the cycle and between two capitals.

And we are using (i, j) with i = (i+1)%n, so each edge is considered once.

In the adjacent_pair_sum, we are adding the product for each such edge.

Yes.

I think this is correct. 

Final code:

Note: we use //2 because (S^2 - T) is even, and for integer values, it should be integer.

Yes, because S^2 - T = 2 * (sum of products for unordered pairs), so it is even.

Therefore, (S^2 - T) // 2 is integer.

We are good.

Let me write the code accordingly. 

One more thing: in the capital_set, we have the 0-indexed indices.

In the edge iteration, i is 0-indexed, and we check if i and j are in capital_set.

All set.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capital_indices = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed and create a set for O(1) lookup
capital_set = set()
for idx in capital_indices:
    capital_set.add(idx - 1)

total_sum = sum(c)

# Compute the sum of products for cycle edges
cycle_edges = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_edges += c[i] * c[j]

# Compute the sum of contributions from each capital to non-adjacent cities
sum_capital_contribution = 0
for x in capital_set:
    # get the two adjacent cities in the cycle (0-indexed)
    prev_idx = (x - 1) % n
    next_idx = (x + 1) % n
    # sum of beauty values of cities not x and not adjacent to x
    other_sum = total_sum - c[x] - c[prev_idx] - c[next_idx]
    sum_capital_contribution += c[x] * other_sum

# Compute total sum of c_i for i in capital_set and sum of squares
S = sum(c[x] for x in capital_set)
T = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capital_set)

# Compute sum of c_i * c_j for adjacent pairs of capitals in the cycle
adjacent_pair_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i in capital_set and j in capital_set:
        adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Total sum of products for all unordered pairs of capitals
total_pair_sum = (S * S - T) // 2

# The sum for non-adjacent capital-capital pairs (which we need to subtract from the overcount)
non_adjacent_capital_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

# Adjust the capital edge contribution: we overcounted by the non-adjacent pairs
capital_edge_sum = sum_capital_contribution - non_adjacent_capital_pair_sum

total_price = cycle_edges + capital_edge_sum
print(total_price)
```

    cycle_edges += c[i] * c[j]

    # get the two adjacent cities in the cycle (0-indexed)
    prev_idx = (x - 1) % n
    next_idx = (x + 1) % n
    # sum of beauty values of cities not x and not adjacent to x
    other_sum = total_sum - c[x] - c[prev_idx] - c[next_idx]
    sum_capital_contribution += c[x] * other_sum

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities
3. Each capital city is directly connected to EVERY other city (including non-capitals)
4. The roads form a cycle (consecutive cities) plus all direct connections from capital cities to other cities
5. The price of passing a road between cities `i` and `j` is `c_i * c_j`
6. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads

## Key Insights

1. **Base cycle roads**: The cities form a cycle 1-2-...-n-1. This gives us roads between (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1).

2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city `x` is connected to every other city `i` (where `i ≠ x`). 

3. **Important observation**: The problem states "there is at most one road between any two cities", so we don't need to double-count roads.

4. **Total price**: We need to sum `c_i * c_j` for all roads.

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate the price from the base cycle
For the cycle roads:
- Between i and i+1: price = c_i * c_{i+1} for i from 1 to n-1
- Between n and 1: price = c_n * c_1

So cycle price = sum of (c_i * c_{i+1}) for i from 1 to n-1 + c_n * c_1

### Step 2: Calculate the price from capital connections
For each capital city `x`, it connects to every other city `i ≠ x`. 
The price contribution from capital `x` is sum of (c_x * c_i) for all i ≠ x.

This equals: c_x * (sum of all c_i) - c_x * c_x = c_x * (total_sum - c_x)

However, we need to be careful: if we just add this for each capital, we might overcount. But actually, each road between a capital and another city is unique, and we're only counting each such road once per capital.

But wait - each capital connection is only counted once when we consider that capital. Since each such road is only between one capital and one other city, there's no overlap.

So total capital price = sum over all capital cities x of [c_x * (total_sum - c_x)]

### Step 3: Total price
Total price = cycle_price + capital_price

Let me verify with the first example:
- n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capitals=[3]
- Cycle roads: (1,2): 2*3=6, (2,3): 3*1=3, (3,4): 1*2=2, (4,1): 2*2=4. Total = 6+3+2+4 = 15
- Capital at city 3 (index 3, beauty=1): connects to cities 1,2,4 with prices: 1*2 + 1*3 + 1*2 = 2+3+2 = 7
- Total = 15 + 7 = 22... but expected is 17.

Hmm, there's an error. Let me reconsider.

Wait - in the base cycle, we already have connections between consecutive cities. The capital connections are additional roads. But the problem says "all cities are consecutively connected" and "each capital city is connected with each other city directly".

So we have both the cycle connections AND the capital connections.

But let me check the example again: cities 1,2,3,4 with beauties 2,3,1,2, capital at city 3.

The cycle gives us: (1,2): 2*3=6, (2,3): 3*1=3, (3,4): 1*2=2, (4,1): 2*2=4. Total = 15.

Capital city 3 connects to: 1,2,4 → prices: 1*2 + 1*3 + 1*2 = 2+3+2 = 7.

But this gives 15+7=22, not 17.

I think I'm double-counting or misunderstanding. Let me re-read.

Actually, looking at the problem: "each capital city is connected with each other city directly". This means there's a direct road from capital to every other city.

3. Add to total

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edges: between i and i+1 for i in 0 to n-2, and between n-1 and 0
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# For each capital city, add edges to non-adjacent cities
capital_contribution = 0
for cap in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in cycle: (cap-1) and (cap+1) mod n
    adjacent = []
    prev = (cap - 1) % n
    next_ = (cap + 1) % n
    adjacent.append(prev)
    adjacent.append(next_)
    
    # Add edge from cap to each city that is not adjacent to cap and not cap itself
    for j in range(n):
        if j != cap and j not in adjacent:
            capital_contribution += c[cap] * c[j]

total = cycle_sum + capital_contribution
print(total)
```

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edges: between i and i+1 for i in 0 to n-2, and between n-1 and 0
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# For each capital city, add edges to non-adjacent cities in the cycle
capital_contribution = 0
for cap in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in cycle: (cap-1) and (cap+1) mod n
    adjacent_set = set()
    prev = (cap - 1) % n
    next_ = (cap + 1) % n
    adjacent_set.add(prev)
    adjacent_set.add(next_)
    
    # Add edge from cap to each city that is not adjacent to cap and not cap itself
    for j in range(n):
        if j != cap and j not in adjacent_set:
            capital_contribution += c[cap] * c[j]

total = cycle_sum + capital_contribution
print(total)
```

View Extracted Code 
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edges: between i and i+1 for i in 0 to n-2, and between n-1 and 0
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# For each capital city, add edges to non-adjacent cities in the cycle
capital_contribution = 0
for cap in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in cycle: (cap-1) and (cap+1) mod n
    adjacent_set = set()
    prev = (cap - 1) % n
    next_ = (cap + 1) % n
    adjacent_set.add(prev)
    adjacent_set.add(next_)
    
    # Add edge from cap to each city that is not adjacent to cap and not cap itself
    for j in range(n):
        if j != cap and j not in adjacent_set:
            capital_contribution += c[cap] * c[j]

total = cycle_sum + capital_contribution
print(total)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities connected in a cyclic route: 1-2-3-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities. Each capital city is directly connected to every other city (including non-capitals)
3. The price of passing a road between cities `i` and `j` is `c_i * c_j`
4. We need to find the sum of products for all existing roads

## Road Types Analysis

There are two types of roads:
1. **Cyclic roads**: Between consecutive cities (i, i+1) for i from 1 to n-1, and between n and 1
2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city is connected to all other cities

However, we need to be careful about double-counting. The problem states "There is at most one road between any two cities", so we don't need to add roads twice.

Alternative insight: 
The total sum is the sum over all pairs (i,j) with i < j of c_i * c_j, but only for edges that exist.

Edges that exist:

2. All edges from capitals to other cities that are not already in the cycle

Yes! Because if we iterate over capital cities in ascending order, and for each capital x, we add c_x * c_y for all y > x such that (x,y) is not a cycle edge, then each edge is added exactly once.

For a capital-capital edge (x,y) with x < y, it will be added once when we consider x.

For a capital-non-capital edge (x,y) with x < y, it will be added once when we consider x.

This covers all edges from capitals to non-adjacent cities.

So the algorithm:

1. Precompute the sum of cycle edges
   - cycle_sum = sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i from 0 to n-1 (using 0-based indexing)

2. For each capital city (in ascending order):
   - For each city y such that y > x and (x,y) is not a cycle edge, add c_x * c_y
   - Note: since we're only adding for y > x, we avoid double counting

3. Return cycle_sum + additional_sum

Now, what defines a cycle edge? 
In the cycle: cities i and i+1 (with i+1 mod n), so edges between consecutive cities.

In 0-based indexing:
- Edge between i and (i+1)%n

For two cities x and y, (x,y) is a cycle edge if |x-y| = 1 (mod n) or if |x-y| = n-1 (i.e., one is n-1 and other is 0).

Actually, in a cycle of n cities, edges are between:
- (0,1), (1,2), ..., (n-2, n-1), (n-1, 0)

So two cities x and y are adjacent in cycle if |x-y| == 1 or |x-y| == n-1 (when one is 0 and other is n-1).

In code, for two indices i and j (0-based), they are adjacent in cycle if abs(i-j) == 1 or (i==0 and j==n-1) or (i==n-1 and j==0).

Since we have x < y in our iteration, we can check if y is adjacent to x in the cycle.

For x < y:
- If y == x+1, then it's a cycle edge
- If y == 0 and x == n-1, but since x < y, this doesn't happen
- Also, if x == 0 and y == n-1, then it's an edge between 0 and n-1

In a cycle, the edge between 0 and n-1 exists.

So for x < y:
- (x,y) is a cycle edge if y == x+1 or (x == 0 and y == n-1)

For example, n=4: cities 0,1,2,3
Cycle edges: (0,1), (1,2), (2,3), (3,0)
With x < y:
- (0,1): y==x+1
- (1,2): y==x+1  
- (2,3): y==x+1
- (3,0): x=3, y=0, but x > y, so not in x<y

So for x < y, only when y == x+1.

But (3,0) is an edge between 0 and 3, which in x<y would be (0,3) with x=0, y=3.

Is (0,3) a cycle edge? Yes, between n-1 and 0.

So for x < y, (x,y) is a cycle edge if y == x+1 or (x == 0 and y == n-1)

But when n>2, and x=0, y=n-1, this is a cycle edge.

So the conditions are:
- y == x+1
- or (x == 0 and y == n-1)

In 0-based indexing, for two indices x and y with x < y, they are adjacent in cycle if:
- y == x + 1
- or (x == 0 and y == n-1)

Yes.

So now I can implement.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Step 1: Calculate sum of cycle edges
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Step 2: Calculate sum of additional edges from capitals to non-adjacent cities
additional_sum = 0

# For each capital city, check all cities y > x that are not adjacent in cycle
for x in capitals:
    for y in range(x + 1, n):  # y > x
        # Check if (x,y) is a cycle edge
        # Cycle edges: (x, x+1) or (0, n-1)
        is_cycle_edge = False
        if y == x + 1:
            is_cycle_edge = True
        elif x == 0 and y == n - 1:
            is_cycle_edge = True
        
        if not is_cycle_edge:
            additional_sum += c[x] * c[y]

# Also need to consider connections from capitals to cities that are not in the range (x+1, n) 
# Wait, we are only iterating y > x, so we cover all pairs with x < y.

# But what about edges between capital and other capital that are not adjacent?
# Example: if we have capitals at 0 and 2, and n=4, edge between 0 and 2 is not a cycle edge (cycle edges are (0,1), (1,2), (2,3), (3,0))
# So (0,2) should be added.

# In our loop: for x=0, y=2 (since 2 > 0), and (0,2) is not a cycle edge (since 2 != 0+1 and 0!=0 and 2!=3), so it will be added.

# So our loop covers all capital-to-other-vertex edges that are not in the cycle.

print(cycle_sum + additional_sum)
```
