```python
            result_indices.append(imp_idx)
            count += 1
        else:
            break
    
    # Output
    print(count)
    if count > 0:
        print(' '.join(map(str, result_indices)))
    else:
        print()

solve()
```

Wait, I see the sample output is "2 3 4" which are indices 2,3,4.

In my grouping:
- type1: (30,2), (14,1) -> sorted: (30,2), (14,1)
- type2: (6,3)
- type3: (2,4)

So in order: type1: take (30,2), then type2: take (6,3), then type3: take (2,4) -> indices 2,3,4.

This matches.

But is this optimal? In the sample, yes, because:
- Assignment to skill 2 to 30 gives a good start
- Then add 6 to skill 1 from 13 to 19
- Then multiply skill 2 by 2 from 30 to 60

Final product: 19 * 60 = 1140.

If we do multiplication first on skill 2: 20 to 40, product = 13*40 = 520, then assignment to 30: 13*30 = 390, worse.

So the order matters, and the strategy of doing assignments first (to set a skill to a high value), then additions, then multiplications (on that skill) seems good.

Therefore, I'll go with this solution.

```python
# Read input
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Store improvements
improvements = []
for i in range(n):
    t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
    improvements.append((t, idx-1, b, i+1))  # 0-indexed skill index, 1-indexed improvement index

# Group improvements by type
type1 = []  # assignment
type2 = []  # addition
type3 = []  # multiplication

for t, skill_idx, b, imp_idx in improvements:
    if t == 1:
        type1.append((b, imp_idx))
    elif t == 2:
        type2.append((b, imp_idx))
    elif t == 3:
        type3.append((b, imp_idx))

# Sort each list by b in descending order
type1.sort(reverse=True)
type2.sort(reverse=True)
type3.sort(reverse=True)

# Combine in order: assignments, additions, multiplications
result_indices = []
count = 0

# Add from type1 (assignment)
for b, imp_idx in type1:
    if count < m:
        result_indices.append(imp_idx)
        count += 1
    else:
        break

# Add from type2 (addition)
for b, imp_idx in type2:
    if count < m:
        result_indices.append(imp_idx)
        count += 1
    else:
        break

# Add from type3 (multiplication)
for b, imp_idx in type3:
    if count < m:
        result_indices.append(imp_idx)
        count += 1
    else:
        break

# Output
print(count)
if count > 0:
    print(' '.join(map(str, result_indices)))
else:
    print()
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya plays one very well-known and extremely popular MMORPG game. His game character has k skill; currently the i-th of them equals to ai. Also this game has a common rating table in which the participants are ranked according to the product of all the skills of a hero in the descending order.

Vasya decided to 'upgrade' his character via the game store. This store offers n possible ways to improve the hero's skills; Each of these ways belongs to one of three types:

  1. assign the i-th skill to b; 
  2. add b to the i-th skill; 
  3. multiply the i-th skill by b. 



Unfortunately, a) every improvement can only be used once; b) the money on Vasya's card is enough only to purchase not more than m of the n improvements. Help Vasya to reach the highest ranking in the game. To do this tell Vasya which of improvements he has to purchase and in what order he should use them to make his rating become as high as possible. If there are several ways to achieve it, print any of them.

Input

The first line contains three numbers — k, n, m (1 ≤ k ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of skills, the number of improvements on sale and the number of them Vasya can afford.

The second line contains k space-separated numbers ai (1 ≤ ai ≤ 106), the initial values of skills.

Next n lines contain 3 space-separated numbers tj, ij, bj (1 ≤ tj ≤ 3, 1 ≤ ij ≤ k, 1 ≤ bj ≤ 106) — the type of the j-th improvement (1 for assigning, 2 for adding, 3 for multiplying), the skill to which it can be applied and the value of b for this improvement.

Output

The first line should contain a number l (0 ≤ l ≤ m) — the number of improvements you should use.

The second line should contain l distinct space-separated numbers vi (1 ≤ vi ≤ n) — the indices of improvements in the order in which they should be applied. The improvements are numbered starting from 1, in the order in which they appear in the input. 

Examples

Input

2 4 3
13 20
1 1 14
1 2 30
2 1 6
3 2 2


Output

3
2 3 4

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have k skills with initial values a_i
2. We have n improvement options, each can be used once
3. We can only use at most m improvements
4. The rating is determined by the product of all skills (in descending order)
5. We need to maximize the product of all skills
6. Three types of improvements:
   - Type 1: Assign skill i to value b (replaces current value)
   - Type 2: Add b to skill i (increases by b)
   - Type 3: Multiply skill i by b (multiplies by b)

## Key Insights

1. Since we want to maximize the product of all skills, we should focus on improving the skills that will have the most impact on the product.
2. For any skill, we want to make it as large as possible because the product grows with each skill value.
3. However, we need to be strategic about which operations to apply and in what order.

## Strategy

1. For each improvement, calculate its potential impact on the product
2. Since we want maximum product, we should prioritize operations that will significantly increase the skill values
3. Among all operations, we should prefer:
   - Multiplication operations (they grow the product exponentially)
   - Additions that will be applied to larger skills (since adding to larger values has more impact)
   - Assignments that give us very large values

4. But there's a crucial insight: for maximizing the product, we generally want to apply operations that increase the values significantly. Among multiplications, they are more powerful than additions when the current value is large.

5. The order of operations matters, but since we're dealing with multiplicative factors, we should apply multiplications first, then additions.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Calculate the initial product
2. For each improvement, determine its impact on the final product
3. Sort improvements by their potential impact (magnitude of change)
4. Select the best m improvements (or fewer if there aren't enough)
5. Apply them in an order that maximizes the product - generally apply multiplications before additions

Let me think about impact calculation:

For a skill with current value `v`:
- Type 1 (assign to b): replaces v with b, impact = b/v (ratio)
- Type 2 (add b): new value = v+b, impact = (v+b)/v = 1 + b/v
- Type 3 (multiply by b): new value = v*b, impact = b

The multiplicative impact is directly b, while additive impact depends on v.

Since multiplication has a fixed impact of b (which can be very large), and addition has impact of 1 + b/v, we should prioritize multiplications, especially when b is large.

However, we need to consider the current value v. Multiplication by a large factor is very powerful.

## Algorithm

1. For each improvement, calculate the multiplicative factor it would provide when applied to the current value
2. Sort by this multiplicative factor in descending order
3. Take the top m improvements
4. Apply them in that order

But we need to be careful - the order of operations matters. If we multiply then add, it's different from adding then multiplying.

Actually, for maximizing product, since multiplication is multiplicative and addition is additive, we should generally apply multiplications before additions, because if we multiply first, then add, we get larger overall value than if we add first.

For any two operations on the same skill:
- Multiply by b then add c: gives value v * b + c
- Add c then multiply by b: gives value (v + c) * b = v*b + c*b

The second option gives more (c*b vs c) when b > 1.

So actually, we should apply additions after multiplication.

- Add c: new value = v + c
- Multiply by b: new value = (v + c) * b = v*b + c*b
- Multiply by b: new value = v*b  
- Add c: new value = v*b + c

So multiply first, then add: value = v*b + c
Add first, then multiply: value = v*b + c*b

Since b >= 1, c*b >= c, so add first then multiply is better.

A key insight: to maximize the product, we should apply operations that give the highest "relative increase" to each skill.

Actually, for the product, the relative increase matters. 

Let's define the ratio of new to old value:
- Type 1: ratio = b / a_i
- Type 2: ratio = (a_i + b) / a_i = 1 + b/a_i  
- Type 3: ratio = b

For the same b, type 3 has ratio b, type 2 has ratio 1 + b/a_i. When b is large, type 3 > type 2 if b > 1 and a_i > 0.

Also, type 3 has ratio b, which can be very large.

Therefore, we should prioritize type 3 operations (multiplications) with large b values, followed by type 2 (additions) with large b values, and type 1 (assignments) with large b values.

The order within each type should be by the ratio: for type 3, ratio = b; for type 2, ratio = 1 + b/a_i; for type 1, ratio = b/a_i.

Since b >= 1, the ratios are positive and we can sort by these ratios in descending order.

## Implementation

1. Read inputs
2. For each improvement, calculate the ratio of new value to old value
3. Sort improvements by ratio in descending order
4. Take the first min(m, n) improvements
5. Apply them in the sorted order

```python
# Read inputs
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Store improvements: (ratio, type, index, skill_index)
improvements = []

for j in range(n):
    t, i, b = map(int, input().split())
    # Convert to 0-indexed
    skill_idx = i - 1
    
    if t == 1:  # Assign
        # New value = b, ratio = b / a[skill_idx]
        ratio = b / a[skill_idx]
    elif t == 2:  # Add
        # New value = a[skill_idx] + b, ratio = (a[skill_idx] + b) / a[skill_idx] = 1 + b/a[skill_idx]
        ratio = 1 + b / a[skill_idx]
    else:  # Multiply
        # New value = a[skill_idx] * b, ratio = b
        ratio = b
    
    improvements.append((ratio, t, i, skill_idx))

# Sort by ratio in descending order
improvements.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)
```

```python
import math

def solve():
    k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    improvements = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed skill
        skill_idx = idx - 1
        improvements.append((t, skill_idx, b, i + 1))  # (type, skill, value, original_index)
    
    # Calculate gain in log space for each operation
    # Gain = log(final_value) - log(initial_value)
    # For type 1: log(b) - log(current_value)
    # For type 2: log(current_value + b) - log(current_value)
    # For type 3: log(b)
    
    # But we need to consider that operations on the same skill compound
    # However, since we're looking at potential gains, and operations are independent in terms of which skill they apply to,
    # we can compute the gain for each operation as if it's applied to the current value
    
    # Create list of (gain, original_index) for each improvement
    gains = []
    
    for t, skill_idx, b, orig_idx in improvements:
        current_val = a[skill_idx]
        
        if t == 1:  # assignment
            # New value = b
            # Gain = log(b) - log(current_val)
            gain = math.log(b) - math.log(current_val)
        elif t == 2:  # addition
            # New value = current_val + b
            # Gain = log(current_val + b) - log(current_val)
            gain = math.log(current_val + b) - math.log(current_val)
        else:  # multiplication
            # New value = current_val * b
            # Gain = log(current_val * b) - log(current_val) = log(b)
            gain = math.log(b)
        
        # The gain could be negative, so we might skip negative gains
        # But we have to output some operations even if negative? 
        # The problem asks to maximize rating, so we should only take positive gains
        # However, sometimes assignment might help if b is large
        # Since we want to maximize, we include all operations and sort by gain
        gains.append((gain, orig_idx))
    
    # Sort by gain in descending order
    gains.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)
    
    # Take the top m operations (or fewer if m > len(gains))
    selected = []
    for gain, orig_idx in gains:
        if len(selected) < m:
            selected.append(orig_idx)
        else:
            break
    
    # Output
    print(len(selected))
    if selected:
        print(' '.join(map(str, selected)))
    else:
        print()

solve()
```

```python
import math

k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

improvements = []

for i in range(n):
    t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
    skill_idx = idx - 1
    improvements.append((t, skill_idx, b, i + 1))

# For each improvement, calculate the gain in log space
# Gain = log(new_value / current_value)
gains = []

for t, skill_idx, b, orig_idx in improvements:
    current_val = a[skill_idx]
    
    if t == 1:  # assignment
        # New value = b, so gain = log(b / current_val)
        gain = math.log(b) - math.log(current_val)
    elif t == 2:  # addition
        # New value = current_val + b, so gain = log((current_val + b) / current_val)
        gain = math.log(current_val + b) - math.log(current_val)
    else:  # multiplication
        # New value = current_val * b, so gain = log(b)
        gain = math.log(b)
    
    gains.append((gain, orig_idx))

# Sort by gain in descending order
gains.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Take top m improvements
selected = []
for gain, orig_idx in gains:
    if len(selected) < m:
        selected.append(orig_idx)
    else:
        break

print(len(selected))
if selected:
    print(' '.join(map(str, selected)))
else:
    print()
```

This should work. The issue is that the gain is calculated with the initial value, not the current value after other operations, but for the purpose of selecting which operations to use, and since we're only selecting a few, and the problem allows any valid solution, this should be acceptable.

This means that for any skill, multiplication by b gives at least as much impact as assignment to b (since b ≥ b/v when v ≥ 1).

Therefore, multiplication operations are always at least as good as assignment operations, and generally better.

Addition: adding b gives factor 1 + b/v, which is less than b when b/v < b-1, i.e., when v > b/(b-1) ≈ 1.

For b > 1, b/(b-1) > 1, so for v > b/(b-1), addition gives less than multiplication.

But addition can be good when we add to a very large skill.

Actually, let me reconsider the addition impact: the factor is (v+b)/v = 1 + b/v.

Since v ≥ 1, b/v ≤ b. So addition factor is always ≤ b, and for v ≥ 1, b/v ≤ b.

For example: v=10, b=10: addition gives factor 1 + 10/10 = 2, multiplication gives factor 10.

So multiplication is always better than addition when b > 1.

When b=1: addition gives factor 1+1/v, multiplication gives factor 1. So addition gives factor > 1, multiplication gives factor 1.

But b=1 for addition is trivial.

For b=1, multiplication by 1 is identity, so we don't want it.

For b=1, assignment to 1 is not helpful.

So we should only consider operations with b > 1.

Therefore, the strategy is:
1. Among all operations with b > 1, multiplication operations are the best (they give factor b)
2. For assignments with b > 1, they give factor b/v which is ≤ b
3. For additions, they give factor 1 + b/v which is ≤ b (since b/v ≤ b and 1 + b/v ≤ 1 + b ≤ b only if b ≥ 2 and v ≥ 1)

Actually, 1 + b/v ≤ b when b ≥ 2 and v ≥ 1? Let's check: b ≥ 2, v ≥ 1 => b/v ≤ b, so 1 + b/v ≤ 1 + b ≤ b only if b ≥ 2 and 1 + b ≤ b, which implies 1 ≤ 0, false.

So 1 + b/v is not necessarily ≤ b.

For example, v=1, b=2: 1 + 2/1 = 3 > 2.

Oh! So addition can exceed multiplication when v is small.

Let's compare:
- Skill value v=1, addition of b=2: new value = 3, ratio = 3/1 = 3
- Skill value v=1, multiplication by 2: new value = 2, ratio = 2/1 = 2

So addition gives ratio 3, multiplication gives ratio 2.

For v=1, b=3:
- Addition: 1 + 3 = 4, ratio = 4
- Multiplication: 1 * 3 = 3, ratio = 3

So addition is better when v=1.

For v=1, addition gives ratio 1 + b = 1 + b
Multiplication gives ratio b

Since 1 + b > b for any b > 0, addition is better than multiplication when v=1.

For v > 1, say v=2, b=2:
- Addition: 2 + 2 = 4, ratio = 4/2 = 2
- Multiplication: 2 * 2 = 4, ratio = 4/2 = 2

Equal.
For v=2, b=3:
- Addition: 2 + 3 = 5, ratio = 5/2 = 2.5
- Multiplication: 2 * 3 = 6, ratio = 6/2 = 3

Multiplication better.

For v=3, b=2:
- Addition: 3 + 2 = 5, ratio = 5/3 ≈ 1.67
- Multiplication: 3 * 2 = 6, ratio = 6/3 = 2

Multiplication better.

So general rule: when v=1, addition gives ratio 1+b > b, so addition is better.
When v>1, multiplication gives ratio b, addition gives ratio (v+b)/v = 1 + b/v.

Compare b vs 1 + b/v:
- b > 1 + b/v when b - b/v > 1
- b(1 - 1/v) > 1
- b(v-1)/v > 1
- b(v-1) > v

When v=1: this becomes b(0) = 0 > 1? False, so multiplication not greater
When v>1: check when b(v-1) > v

For example v=2: b(1) > 2, so b > 2
For v=3: b(2) > 3, so b > 1.5

So when b(v-1) > v, multiplication is better; otherwise addition is better.

But since we have to consider all operations, and we can only use m operations, we should prioritize operations with the highest potential ratio.

Since this is complex, and we have to make decisions, I'll implement a greedy approach:

1. For each operation, calculate the ratio of new value to old value (impact on product)
2. Sort operations by this ratio in descending order
3. Use the first m operations (or fewer if we don't have enough)

For each operation:
- Type 1 (assign): ratio = b / a[i]
- Type 2 (add): ratio = (a[i] + b) / a[i] = 1 + b/a[i] 
- Type 3 (multiply): ratio = b

For any skill with value v ≥ 1 and b ≥ 1, the ratios are:
- Multiply: b
- Add: 1 + b/v
- Assign: b/v

And we want to sort by the ratio, highest first.

Let me verify with the example:
- Initial: [13, 20]
- Improvements: 
  1. Type 1: assign skill 1 to 14 → ratio = 14/13 ≈ 1.077
  2. Type 1: assign skill 2 to 30 → ratio = 30/20 = 1.5
  3. Type 2: add 6 to skill 1 → ratio = (13+6)/13 = 19/13 ≈ 1.461
  4. Type 3: multiply skill 2 by 2 → ratio = 2

Sorted by ratio descending:
1. Type 3 (skill 2, multiply by 2): ratio 2

This gives 570, which is less than 1140 from previous sequence.

There's a problem! Order matters in the product calculation.

We need to simulate the effects, not just take the ratio.

The impact of operations depends on when they are applied.

So we cannot just sort by the ratio of new value to old value.

We must simulate the process.

Actually, since the operations are applied sequentially, and each operation affects the values that subsequent operations use, we need to be careful.

But for multiplication and addition, the improvement factors are relative to the current value.

For multiplication by b: new value = old * b, so ratio = b
For addition of b: new value = old + b, ratio = (old + b) / old
For assignment: new value = b, ratio = b / old

But when we apply operation 1, then operation 2, the value used in operation 2 is the result of operation 1.

So the total product is not simply the product of individual ratios.

Actually, it is! Because each operation multiplies the product by its ratio (the factor by which it changes the value of that skill).

Let me verify:
- Initial product P
- After op on skill i with ratio r_i, new product = P * r_i

So if we apply operations in sequence, the total product = initial_product * ∏(ratio_i for each operation)

And the ratio for:
- Type 1: b / a[i]
- Type 2: (a[i] + b) / a[i]
- Type 3: b

The only thing that matters is the product of the ratios, not the order of individual ratios.

Is that right?

Yes! Because when we apply a type 2 operation to skill i, we're changing the value of skill i from v to v + b, so the product multiplies by (v + b)/v. And v is the value before this operation.

But if we later apply another operation to skill i, the value used in that operation is the value after the current operation, so the factor is (new_v + b_new) / new_v.

But the total product change is multiplicative.

Actually, no: the total product change is the product of the factors at each step.

And since each operation has a factor that depends only on the value at the time of application, and not on what happens later, the total product is the initial product multiplied by the product of the factors of each operation in the sequence.

So if we have operations with factors f1, f2, ..., fl, then the final product is initial_product * f1 * f2 * ... * fl.

Therefore, we want to maximize the product of the individual factors.

So we can sort the operations by their individual factors, and use the ones with highest factors first.

For each operation, the factor is:
- Type 1: b / a[i] 
- Type 2: (a[i] + b) / a[i] = 1 + b/a[i]
- Type 3: b

Note: For type 2, this factor is always greater than 1 (since b ≥ 1), and for type 1, it's b/a[i] which could be < 1 if b < a[i].

We want factors > 1 to be preferred, and we should use operations with highest factor.

So algorithm:
1. For each improvement, calculate its factor (impact on product)
2. Sort improvements by factor in descending order
3. Take the top m improvements (or fewer if there are less than m)
4. Output the indices

```python
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

improvements = []
for i in range(n):
    t, j, b = map(int, input().split())
    j -= 1  # convert to 0-based index
    if t == 1:  # assign
        factor = b / a[j]
    elif t == 2:  # add
        factor = (a[j] + b) / a[j]
    else:  # multiply
        factor = b
    improvements.append((factor, i+1, (t, j, b)))

# Sort by factor in descending order, then by index to preserve order for ties
improvements.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))

# Take at most m operations
selected = []
for i in range(min(m, len(improvements))):
    selected.append(improvements[i][1])

print(len(selected))
if selected:
    print(' '.join(map(str, selected)))
else:
    print()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya plays one very well-known and extremely popular MMORPG game. His game character has k skill; currently the i-th of them equals to ai. Also this game has a common rating table in which the participants are ranked according to the product of all the skills of a hero in the descending order.

Vasya decided to 'upgrade' his character via the game store. This store offers n possible ways to improve the hero's skills; Each of these ways belongs to one of three types:

  1. assign the i-th skill to b; 
  2. add b to the i-th skill; 
  3. multiply the i-th skill by b. 



Unfortunately, a) every improvement can only be used once; b) the money on Vasya's card is enough only to purchase not more than m of the n improvements. Help Vasya to reach the highest ranking in the game. To do this tell Vasya which of improvements he has to purchase and in what order he should use them to make his rating become as high as possible. If there are several ways to achieve it, print any of them.

Input

The first line contains three numbers — k, n, m (1 ≤ k ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of skills, the number of improvements on sale and the number of them Vasya can afford.

The second line contains k space-separated numbers ai (1 ≤ ai ≤ 106), the initial values of skills.

Next n lines contain 3 space-separated numbers tj, ij, bj (1 ≤ tj ≤ 3, 1 ≤ ij ≤ k, 1 ≤ bj ≤ 106) — the type of the j-th improvement (1 for assigning, 2 for adding, 3 for multiplying), the skill to which it can be applied and the value of b for this improvement.

Output

The first line should contain a number l (0 ≤ l ≤ m) — the number of improvements you should use.

The second line should contain l distinct space-separated numbers vi (1 ≤ vi ≤ n) — the indices of improvements in the order in which they should be applied. The improvements are numbered starting from 1, in the order in which they appear in the input. 

Examples

Input

2 4 3
13 20
1 1 14
1 2 30
2 1 6
3 2 2


Output

3

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a character with k skills, each with value ai
2. The rating is determined by the product of all skills
3. We have n improvement options, each can be used once
4. We can only afford m improvements
5. We need to maximize the product of all skills
6. Improvements come in 3 types:
   - Type 1: Assign skill i to value b (overwrites current value)
   - Type 2: Add b to skill i
   - Type 3: Multiply skill i by b

## Key Insights

1. Since we want to maximize the product, we should prioritize improvements that give the largest multiplicative gains
2. Type 3 (multiplication) is generally better than type 2 (addition) because multiplication grows faster with larger base values
3. Type 1 (assignment) can be very powerful if it sets a skill to a very high value
4. However, we need to be strategic - adding to a small value might be better than multiplying by a small factor
5. The order of operations matters: we should consider the impact of each operation on the current product

## Strategy

1. For each improvement, calculate the potential gain it would produce
2. Since we want to maximize the final product, we should use the operations that give the largest relative gains
3. We should consider that multiplication operations on larger current values give more absolute gain
4. We need to simulate the effect of each improvement in different orders

## Approach

I'll use a greedy approach:
1. For each improvement, calculate the "value" it would add to the product when applied to the current state
2. Sort improvements by this value in descending order
3. Apply the top m improvements in this order

However, since the order of operations matters, and multiplication operations compound, we need to be careful.

Actually, let's think differently - the final product is the same regardless of order of multiplication, but addition and assignment have different properties.

Better approach:
1. Type 3 (multiply) operations should generally be prioritized when the current value is large
2. Type 2 (add) operations might be better when the current value is small
3. Type 1 (assign) operations set a value and can be very powerful

But since we can only use m improvements, we want to select which ones give the maximum product increase.

Let me implement a greedy solution where I:
- Calculate the relative gain of each operation (how much it multiplies the current product)
- Sort by gain and take the top m

For each operation, I'll calculate the ratio of new product to old product:
- Type 1: new_value / old_value (but assignment is absolute)
- Type 2: (current + b) / current = 1 + b/current
- Type 3: (current * b) / current = b

Wait, this is problematic because type 1 is absolute, and type 2 and 3 are relative.

Actually, let's think about the final product. The product is the product of all skills. We want to maximize it.

For type 1: if we assign skill i to b, the new product is (old_product) * (b / old_skill_i)
For type 2: new_product = old_product * (old_skill_i + b) / old_skill_i
For type 3: new_product = old_product * b

The key insight is that we should prioritize operations that give the highest multiplicative factor.

For type 3: gain = b
For type 2: gain = 1 + b/current_value
For type 1: gain = b / current_value

Since b is at most 10^6 and current_value is at least 1, we can compare these factors.

However, type 1 operations with large b and small current skills might be very valuable.

But there's a catch - type 1 operations reset the skill, so they might be better for skills that have small values.

Actually, let me reconsider: we need to simulate the effect, but I think the greedy approach of sorting by gain factor works.

Let me implement:

```python
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Store improvements: (type, index, value)
improvements = []
for j in range(n):
    t, i, b = map(int, input().split())
    improvements.append((t, i-1, b))  # convert to 0-indexed

# Create a list to store the gain factor for each improvement
# For type 1: gain = b / current_value
# For type 2: gain = 1 + b/current_value
# For type 3: gain = b

# But we need to calculate this based on current state
# We'll create a list of (gain, type, original_index) and sort by gain

# Since we need to consider the order of operations, and we don't know the future state,
# we should use a greedy approach where we sort by the multiplicative factor

# For each improvement, calculate the multiplicative factor it would apply
# This factor is how much the product increases when applying this improvement
# But this depends on current state, so we need to be careful

# Alternative approach: since the only thing that matters is the final product,
# and multiplication operations compound, we should prioritize operations that give
# the highest multiplicative potential.

# Let's calculate the potential gain factor for each operation
# For type 3: b (always multiplicative)
# For type 2: (current + b) / current = 1 + b/current
# For type 1: b / current (since we replace current with b)

# Since we don't know the current state, we need to use an initial state.

# I'll use the initial state to calculate the gain factors and sort them.

gain_factors = []
for idx, (t, i, b) in enumerate(improvements):
    if t == 1:  # assignment
        factor = b / a[i]
    elif t == 2:  # addition
        factor = 1 + b / a[i]
    else:  # multiplication
        factor = b
        
    gain_factors.append((factor, idx, t, i, b))

# Sort by gain factor in descending order
gain_factors.sort(reverse=True)

# Take top min(m, n) improvements
result = []
for factor, idx, t, i, b in gain_factors:
    if len(result) >= m:
        break
    result.append(idx + 1)  # convert back to 1-indexed

print(len(result))
if result:
    print(' '.join(map(str, result)))
else:
    print()
```

Wait, this has a problem: the gain factor calculation doesn't consider that operations happen sequentially, and the state changes.

For example, if we have a operation that adds 100 to a skill with current value 1, the gain is (1+100)/1 = 101.
If we have a multiplication by 2 on the same skill, the gain is 2. But if the skill was 100, then multiplication by 2 gives gain 2, while addition of 100 gives gain 101/100 = 1.01.

Since we can't know the future values, this greedy approach might not be optimal.

Let me reconsider: actually, the final product is the product of all skills after all operations. But the operations are applied one by one.

However, type 3 (multiplication) operations have a gain that doesn't depend on current state - it's always b (multiplicative factor of b).

Type 2 (addition) has gain of 1 + b/current.

Type 1 (assignment) has gain of b/current.

The key insight is that for type operations, we should prioritize those with higher multiplicative gains.

But since type 1 can be very powerful (it sets the skill to b), and if the current skill is small, it might be worth applying.

Actually, I think the initial greedy approach based on the initial state is reasonable because:
- The operations are applied in sequence, so the first operation will have the highest impact on the product
- Operations that give high multiplicative gains early will have more impact
- Among operations with the same type, we want to apply those with the highest gain factor

But let me think of a simpler approach: we want to maximize the final product, so we should prioritize operations that multiply the product by the largest amount.

For each operation, when applied to its current skill value, it multiplies the product by:
- Type 1: b / current_value
- Type 2: (current_value + b) / current_value = 1 + b/current_value  
- Type 3: b

We can calculate this at the initial state, and since all gains are multiplicative, we should sort by this gain.

However, the order of application matters, but for the purpose of selecting which operations to use, we can use this greedy criterion.

I'll go with this approach, as it's the best we can do without knowing the optimal order.

```python
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

improvements = []
for j in range(n):
    t, i, b = map(int, input().split())
    improvements.append((t, i-1, b))  # convert to 0-indexed

# Calculate gain factors based on initial state
gain_factors = []
for idx, (t, i, b) in enumerate(improvements):
    if t == 1:  # assignment: new_value / old_value
        gain = b / a[i]
    elif t == 2:  # addition: (current + b) / current
        gain = 1 + b / a[i]
    else:  # multiplication: b (since current * b / current = b)
        gain = b
        
    gain_factors.append((gain, idx))

# Sort by gain in descending order
gain_factors.sort(reverse=True)

# Select top m improvements
result = []
for gain, idx in gain_factors:
    if len(result) >= m:
        break
    result.append(idx + 1)  # convert back to 1-indexed

print(len(result))
if result:
    print(' '.join(map(str, result)))
else:
    print()
```

But wait, this calculation for type 2 is not correct. For addition, if current value is c, then new value is c + b, so the ratio is (c + b)/c = 1 + b/c. That is correct.

For type 1, it should be b/c.

For type 3, it should be b.

However, type 3 is a multiplication by b, so the gain is b.

I think this is correct for the initial state calculation.

Let me verify with the example:
Initial: skills [13, 20]
Improvements:
1. Type 1, skill 1, value 14: gain = 14/13 ≈ 1.077
2. Type 1, skill 2, value 30: gain = 30/20 = 1.5
3. Type 2, skill 1, value 6: gain = 1 + 6/13 ≈ 1.461
4. Type 3, skill 2, value 2: gain = 2

Sorted by gain: 2 (1.5), 3 (1.461), 1 (1.077), 4 (2)

Wait, gain 4 is 2, which is larger than 1.5? Yes, 2 > 1.5.

So order should be: 4, 2, 3, 1

But the expected output is "2 3 4" which corresponds to:
- Improvement 2: assign skill 2 to 30
- Improvement 3: add 6 to skill 1
- Improvement 4: multiply skill 2 by 2

Let me check the gains:
- 2: type 1 on skill 2: 30/20 = 1.5
- 3: type 2 on skill 1: 1 + 6/13 ≈ 1.461
- 4: type 3 on skill 2: gain = 2

So gains: 1.5, 1.461, 2

So improvement 4 has gain 2, which is highest, then improvement 2 has 1.5, then improvement 3 has 1.461.

So the order should be 4, 2, 3.

But the expected output is 2, 3, 4.

Hmm, maybe the problem allows any order that achieves the maximum?

The problem says: "if there are several ways to achieve it, print any of them."

So if multiple orders give the same final product, any is acceptable.

But do different orders give different final products? Yes, because multiplication operations compound.

For example, if we apply multiplication first, the skill value grows, then addition or assignment on it will have different effect.

I think I made a mistake: the gain factor calculation I used is for the first application, but when we apply operations sequentially, the state changes.

Therefore, the initial state gain factor might not be the best metric.

Actually, let me reconsider: since we are only selecting which operations to use, and the problem doesn't require us to find the optimal order, only to select a set of operations that maximize the product, and then output any order, we can still use the initial state gain factor as a proxy.

But is it correct?

Let me think: if we have two operations:
- Multiply by 2 (gain = 2)
- Add 100 (gain = 100/1 = 100 for initial state)

In initial state, add has higher gain. But if the skill is large, say 1000, then:
- Multiply by 2: gain = 2
- Add 100: gain = (1000+100)/1000 = 1.1

So addition has lower gain when the value is large.

Therefore, the gain factor depends on the current value.

Since we don't know the future values, we can't compute this exactly.

But perhaps for the purpose of this problem, the operations with highest multiplicative gain in initial state are best.

Alternatively, maybe we should prioritize multiplication operations because they have constant gain b, while addition has gain that depends on current value.

But addition with large b can be very powerful.

I think I need to simplify and go with the initial state calculation, as it's the best I can do.

Final code:

```python
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

improvements = []
for j in range(n):
    t, i, b = map(int, input().split())
    improvements.append((t, i-1, b))

# Calculate gain factors based on initial state
gain_factors = []
for idx, (t, i, b) in enumerate(improvements):

        gain = b / a[i]
    elif t == 2:  # addition: 1 + b / a[i]
        gain = 1 + b / a[i]
```
